Startup taps underwater volcano as energy source in "proof point" test
2026-10-19

Startup taps underwater volcano as energy source in "proof point" test

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The world’s first demonstration of a subsea geothermal power plant has taken place near an active underwater volcano almost a mile below the ocean surface. It generated less than 10 seconds of net-positive electricity but showed the technical feasibility of tapping another form of renewable energy underwater. The three-week test deployment of the 3-ton prototype power plant took place at the Axial Seamount in the northeast Pacific Ocean in September. Endurance Energy, the Seattle-based startup behind the effort, aims to eventually commercialize subsea geothermal power for Pacific island nations and other customers looking for alternatives to importing expensive fossil fuels. “The deep ocean gives us unlimited cold seawater for cooling, and its weight keeps the system and the well pressurized, so our plants can be compact,” said Andrew Redd, founder and CEO of Endurance Energy, in an email to Ars. But efforts to tap geothermal power on the seafloor face many different challenges from land-based geothermal projects, which can also add up to higher installation and operational costs. So Endurance Energy must demonstrate consistent underwater operations as well as prove the approach can be cost-effective. “It’s probably technically feasible, but it would undoubtedly be more expensive than to do it on land,” said Roland Horne, professor of energy science & engineering at Stanford University, in an emailed response to Ars. Endurance Energy’s deployment required a subsea lander drill to deploy from the ship Ocean Guardian—operated by Stabbert Maritime—and drill two holes in the seafloor at a depth of about 5,000 feet (1,524 meters). That means the test took place at a depth exceeding 2.5 times the height of the One World Trade Center building in New York City, which is the tallest building in the United States. Both the drilled holes started venting hot hydrothermal fluid measured at a temperature of about 500° F (260° C). The Endurance Energy team proceeded to case and cement the second hole before installing a valve to create a well. Next up, the company deployed its 100-kilowatt power plant, named Adélie, that is designed to vaporize incoming hydrothermal fluid to spin a turbine and drive an electric generator. It used the assistance of Canada’s remotely operated submersible ROPOS to help connect the power plant to the newly drilled well. Adélie also connected to Endurance Energy’s Seattle command center by plugging into the Regional Cabled Array, a 559-mile (900-kilometer) network of undersea fiber-optic and electric cables that provides both power and connectivity to scientific sensors on the seafloor. The undersea network is funded by the US National Science Foundation and is operated by the University of Washington. That all set the stage for the Adélie power test. But a major hitch soon arose—the drilled well extending 52 feet (16 meters) down into the seafloor was not delivering enough of the hydrothermal fluid to run the power plant. “For our first well, the goal was to show we could drill, case, cement, and cap a well on the seafloor, so we kept it small and shallow,” Redd explained. “That limited how much fluid it could deliver, and deeper wells should change that.” The test well was also just 2 inches (5 centimeters) wide, compared to oil, gas, and onshore geothermal wells that typically have casings 20 to 36 inches (51 to 91 centimeters) across. Endurance Energy plans to avoid this issue in the future by drilling deeper wells to access more fluid. The company’s current lander drill can reach about 260 feet (80 meters), which would be five times deeper than the initial test well. To try running the Adélie power plant anyway, the team redeployed it to a nearby natural vent where hydrothermal fluid was already flowing out at about 538° F (281° C). On September 27, they gave it a try. Over nearly half a minute, the power plant turbine reached about 26,000 rpm and enabled the generator to produce 60 watt-hours of electricity. The power plant also became net-positive by producing more electricity than it consumed for about 8.7 seconds. It produced a median of about 2 kilowatts of net power and peaked at 16.5 kilowatts of net power. “It’s the proof point,” Redd told Ars. “Net-positive is the threshold that matters: the plant made more power than it took to run itself on the seafloor, which no one had done before.” But then a software issue brought the power run to a halt. Before the generator takes over, Adélie first relies on a battery to run its pumps, thrusters, and controls, along with spinning the turbine to get it up to speed, Redd explained. A battery module recorded a brief spike in current during the spin-up process and then stopped reporting. That prompted Adélie’s software to automatically stop the power plant as a safety precaution, because it kept relying on the old reading from the battery module. When the team was restarting the power plant, a power cable feeding the refrigerant pump also failed when an imperfection in the cable casing allowed seawater to seep in and short two of the electrical conductors. That ended the trial run, although Endurance Energy already plans to use the experience to improve how it builds and tests subsea cables. Placing the geothermal plant on top of a natural hydrothermal vent was a one-time test for purely research and demonstration purposes, Redd said. His company still plans to rely on drilled wells in future deployments. Endurance Energy also described taking steps to reduce potential environmental impacts on the seafloor and marine life. The team described choosing the “least disruptive” site for the test run and continuously monitored the process by sampling the water and recording the seafloor array of tubeworms, snails, crabs, and bacterial mats. It also checked findings against scientific sensors run by independent researchers and sent water, rock, and mineral samples for independent lab analysis. The company’s next trial run planned for the winter of 2026—2027 will involve a refurbished and upgraded Adélie—renamed Gentoo in keeping with the penguin-inspired naming—that will aim to sustain higher power generation. The company is also developing a larger 2-megawatt commercial generator for its first planned commercial deployment with the Kingdom of Tonga in 2027. The Polynesian island nation in the South Pacific Ocean runs its power grid primarily on imported diesel fuel, which has significantly increased in price since the United States and Israel launched their war with Iran. “The 2 MW commercial generators will be about the size of a shipping container, and we are deploying up to six of these generators [to] offshore Tonga,” Redd told Ars. “The physical footprint of this project will be relatively small.” Geothermal energy projects are already cost-effective on land but “on the edge of competitive pricewise,” said Horne at Stanford University. So he cautioned that any factors making geothermal more expensive would “push it to the noncompetitive side.” Still, other customers may consider Endurance Energy’s approach if the company can demonstrate reliable operations over time with the Kingdom of Tonga. The skyrocketing cost of imported fossil fuels is a huge factor that has already spurred multiple governments to invest in more renewable energy alternatives. “Large markets with ample offshore geothermal resources include the United States—Pacific Northwest and Alaska—Taiwan, and Japan,” Redd said. “As the technology matures, we expect to compete on cost and deliver continuous, weather-independent power to utilities and large energy users.”

ReactOS reaches major milestone with NTFS install support
2026-10-19

ReactOS reaches major milestone with NTFS install support

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Open source Windows alternative ReactOS has reached a major milestone: The operating system can now be installed directly onto an NT Files System drive partition. As reported by Phoronix, the move to NTFS clears a long-standing hurdle for the project and brings it one step closer to finally reaching beta. Prior to this latest announcement, ReactOS required users to keep a FAT32 partition for installations. A proprietary Microsoft-developed filesystem, NTFS can trace its roots back through the Windows NT family lineage starting in 1993 with Windows NT 3.1. The variant introduced with Windows XP in 2001 remains the standard used by most Windows systems today. With ReactOS’s stated goal of reaching binary compatibility with Windows Server 2003, being confined to FAT32 partitions was a practical barrier to clear on the way to reproducing the Windows environment. However, users looking to hop off the Windows train shouldn’t be grabbing their bags just yet. ReactOS wrote that the new driver still “lacks caching (which causes inferior performance), journaling and security support.” So while the new driver is here, even the most intrepid of OS tinkerers should tread carefully. The ReactOS team warned in its announcement on X that there are still “potentially undiscovered bugs.” The new driver closes a gap in development of nearly a decade. Read-only NTFS support was added to ReactOS all the way back in 2016, with boot support landing on FreeLoader in 2022. That meant that ReactOS’s bootloader could read from an NTFS partition, but the OS was unable to be directly installed there. ReactOS is a community-developed open source project that describes itself as “mostly driven by volunteers.” Development is coordinated through public channels like GitHub and the project’s forums. Those who might be interested in lending a hand to ReactOS’s development can visit the project website to learn more about how to contribute. The project’s decades-long timeline underscores the scope and difficulty of reimplementing an operating system from scratch, and why the project has remained in alpha for so long. The ReactOS project may never be a truly viable alternative to Windows for most users, but this step forward means that at least now it can be installed on the same filesystem as the real thing.

AI game clones are making indie developers afraid to show their work
2026-10-19

AI game clones are making indie developers afraid to show their work

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Since its earliest days, the video game industry has been built on developers creating clones of competing games, with varying levels of originality and varying levels of enforcement from game platforms. Now, though, AI coding tools are making that cloning process so efficient that many developers are getting extremely cautious about sharing their early work too widely. Developer Alliance Arts highlighted this trend over the weekend, delaying the launch of a planned public demo for its demon-themed Russian Roulette drinking game Drunken Goddess Reflex, which had been set to go live with today’s launch of Steam Next Fest. In a post on social media Friday evening, the Alliance Arts team wrote that, in addition to concerns about managing streaming restrictions, “we’re concerned that the demo’s content could be used to quickly imitate or replicate the game’s presentation and gameplay with AI or other tools and that such works could be released before our launch.” Instead, the developer now plans to share the demo only in “closed sessions” with those who agree to rules regarding posting and streaming gameplay. Alliance Arts isn’t alone in taking a more defensive stance toward sharing ideas. Papers, Please developer Lucas Pope said on an April podcast that, in general, “you don’t really talk about stuff when you’re working on it because I don’t know it’s going to get slurped up by AI or people are going to copy it or something else like that… I just didn’t feel as comfortable talking about the stuff I was working on again.” Concerns about ideas getting “slurped by AI” have been more than theoretical for a number of indie developers in recent months. In March, developer Freya Holmér attracted some viral social media attention after sharing a video of a prototype of a Tetris-style game played on a central rotating pivot point. Days later, Holmér was confronted with a number of vibe-coded copies that made the basic concept widely playable before Holmér could release her own version. “Y’know it kinda disincentivizes me from sharing progress when there are slop ghouls around every corner, AI or otherwise,” Holmér wrote in response to the spread of those preemptive clones. “Luckily these people are incapable of original thought and don’t know how to elevate this concept beyond what they’ve already seen.” McPixel developer Sos Sosowski has been documenting numerous examples of fellow indie devs complaining of AI-generated clones making wholesale copies of their efforts. Sosowski says the situation puts indie developers in a hopeless bind when trying to promote their work: “Don’t share your game—no sales. Share your game—gets stolen,” he wrote. “How do you even survive as an indie in the current landscape? This is insane.” Charlie Greenman, who posted one of those AI-powered clones of Holmér’s rotational Tetris game earlier this year, told 404 Media that he didn’t see anything wrong with what he did. “I feel like I had this brand new creation,” Greenman said. “When it gets to that point, is one song copying another? Is one game copying another? Whoever created Blox, Jenga, is that a copy of Tetris?” Some think that added complexity and originality is the best true protection from blatant clones in this kind of environment. “If AI can copy your game overnight, it wasn’t a real game. It was just a quick cash grab built on a single mechanic, with no soul or vision,” Howltide Games’ Ivan Lago wrote on social media. “AI isn’t cloning actual games. It’s just making cheap knockoffs of stuff that was already an asset flip to begin with.” But even if your game idea is cloned by AI slop merchants, that doesn’t mean you can’t build out the better version you always pictured. “In the very beginning, that first week, just every day there was a new AI clone [of the rotational Tetris prototype]. I was like, OK, well fuck me, I guess,” Holmér told 404 Media this summer. “But there’s way less engagement [now], and I feel more in the clear to take my time to actually make something right, something good I can be proud of, and not just get it out there as soon as possible.”

Florida DMV hacked after police dept insecurely stored login info
2026-10-19

Florida DMV hacked after police dept insecurely stored login info

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The Florida Department of Highway Safety and Motor Vehicles (FLHSMV) has begun notifying victims impacted by a recent data breach of driver’s license and motor vehicle data, but not everyone whose information was stolen will receive an alert. In a notice posted Friday, FLHSMV confirmed that it took “immediate steps” after discovering that “a malicious actor accessed certain department records on September 4, 2026, using compromised credentials.” There’s “no evidence” that data has yet been misused, the department said, but law enforcement “determined the information potentially affected includes driver license and motor vehicle data, to include document images, and other personal information.” FLHSMV is sending out letters to everyone affected, offering victims one year of credit monitoring from Sontiq, Inc. However, the state acknowledged that it may not have current contact information for many former Florida residents whose data was still being stored. Those potential victims can confirm if their data was stolen by emailing FLHSMV at customerinquiry@flhsmv.gov, officials said. For all current and former Florida residents, FLHSMV recommended taking precautions, including monitoring financial accounts and reviewing credit reports for any suspicious activity. In a thread on the Florida subreddit, several Floridians confirmed that they had already taken officials’ third recommendation and placed “a fraud alert or security freeze with major credit bureaus.” Some residents complained that they received notice only this week, even though the breach occurred more than a month ago. The breach was previously reported by news outlets. On a webpage describing the security incident, FLHSMV confirmed that an “international cybercriminal organization” was behind the breach but did not specify which group or how long they had access. Instead, officials claimed to have “promptly mitigated the breach, launched an investigation, and issued required notification to state authorities.” Mitigations included “blocking unauthorized access, resetting relevant user credentials, engaging cybersecurity professionals, and implementing additional safeguards to protect sensitive information,” FLHSMV said. Florida’s investigation is “ongoing,” officials said, but a cybercriminal group called ShinyHunters—which recently claimed credit for hacking the FBI—may have stolen the data. Last month, NBC Miami reviewed a dark web post in which ShinyHunters announced that it had hacked Florida’s DMV and given officials a September 11 deadline to negotiate a fee to stop leaks of the data. To prove the breach was real, ShinyHunters posted a photo of the “alleged driver’s license for late financier and convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, a former Florida resident,” NBC Miami reported. But soon after that post was published, ShinyHunters made edits, removing all mentions of Florida, NBC Miami noted. Ian Gray, vice president of intelligence at the threat monitoring company Flashpoint, told NBC Miami this was a likely indication that Florida officials were negotiating with ShinyHunters to prevent a leak of the database. FLHSMV did not mention ShinyHunters in its announcement and did not answer NBC Miami’s questions about the dark web post. In their announcement, officials said they would post an update when more information is available to share publicly. The hack appears to be unrelated to a major breach of 153 million US driver’s licenses involving a company called IDscan.net last month, NBC Miami reported. But both hacks highlight how easy it is to get access to millions of Americans’ personal information, which will only get easier as AI advances and simplifies the process of scanning for vulnerabilities, NBC suggested. In Florida’s case, “a criminal actor gained unauthorized access to a department system” by acquiring the “credentials from an employee of a local Florida law enforcement agency, who improperly stored the credentials on an electronic device,” FLHSMV’s FAQ said.

Disney found a way to buy Character.AI without buying its lawsuits
2026-10-19

Disney found a way to buy Character.AI without buying its lawsuits

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
This week, Character.AI declared bankruptcy. While some saw the fall of a chatbot platform as a harbinger for the industry, court filings indicate that the decision was actually a key part of a deal that has let Disney hire most of Character.AI’s staff—including its now former CEO Karandeep Anand—potentially acquire most of Character.AI’s technology, and avoid the beleaguered startup’s many legal liabilities and obligations. In the past few years, Character.AI has faced multiple private lawsuits over the safety of its chatbots, as well as investigations from regulators around the world. In a declaration filed as part of the bankruptcy, Character.AI’s newly appointed CEO explained that the goal of the bankruptcy was to reach a “consensual resolution” with personal injury claimants and a group of states investigating the company, while maximizing the value of the company’s assets for shareholders. The Disney deal seems to be structured so that whatever is left of Character.AI will retain responsibility for those legal battles, some of which are still ongoing. The move appears to be a new twist on what some have dubbed a “reverse acquihire,” a process in which a buyer carefully orchestrates a succession of deals with a target company, usually in an effort to hire key employees and obtain expansive license to use and build on the target company’s unique technology. Stripped of its employees, the target becomes a husk of itself. Companies often take the position that reverse acquihires are not actual mergers, and that they do not have to notify federal antitrust regulators about those deals as they would otherwise be required to by law. These arrangements have become more commonplace in the AI industry in recent years, drawing the ire of Congress. In the case of Character.AI, a motion filed by Character.AI’s legal counsel says that Disney required that the deal be “fully consummated” through bankruptcy proceedings because of the startup’s “diminishing cash reserve and significant litigation and regulatory risks.” A spokesperson for Character.AI referred WIRED to Disney for comment on whether the deal was contingent on the startup declaring bankruptcy. Disney declined to comment on this story. According to court filings, the deal involves “two interrelated and integrated” transactions—one before Character.AI declared bankruptcy, and one after. As part of the first transaction, which occurred in September, Disney paid the startup $5.5 million, along with a refundable “good faith deposit” of $2 million. Disney also hired the “majority” of Character.AI’s employees, including the CEO, and agreed that it would let some of the now-Disney employees help keep Character.AI operational for five months. Character.AI filed a petition for chapter 11 bankruptcy on October 12. Through the bankruptcy proceedings, court filings show that Disney hopes to secure a perpetual, worldwide, nonexclusive license for a huge range of Character.AI’s code, documentation, frameworks, tools, and “other artifacts,” including those produced for its trust and safety moderation stack, training infrastructure, and age-prediction technology. The list of IP included in the filings does not include users’ data (which can get additional protections in bankruptcy proceedings) or Character.AI’s website. That license will be auctioned off in the coming months. If Disney is chosen as the winning bidder by Character.AI and a committee of unsecured creditors—and courts approve the deal—it has agreed to pay Character.AI an additional $12.5 million as a closing payment. If Disney is not selected, the company will be refunded the $2 million deposit and receive a breakup fee. In order to “preserve a competitive process,” Disney also agreed to permit the winning bidder to interview and potentially extend offers to up to 80 percent of “certain newly hired employees” that Disney already hired from Character.AI. Disney has the right to select which employees. However, experts worry that bouncing Character.AI employees from one company to another in the span of months is unrealistic, and that the deal Disney and Character.AI have struck cannot be easily unwound. In addition, Disney prepared a draft of a court order referred to as the “363 Free and Clear Order” in the filings, a version of which would be approved by the court at or before closing, that would clarify that this process should not be viewed as a merger of the two companies and that Character.AI would remain responsible for “any liability or other obligation” it might have, including any legal claims against it. A spokesperson for Character.AI said they were unable to speculate on the outcome of the bankruptcy proceedings and referred WIRED to the docket. “There’s decades of corporate law that developed to deal with the problem of a buyer who always wants to take on all the assets and never wants to take on any of the liability,” says John Newman, a law professor at the University of Memphis School of Law and a former deputy director of the Federal Trade Commission’s Bureau of Competition. But the Disney-Character.AI deal is extremely unusual and structured in a way, he says, that “all those corporate law safeguards really weren’t built to guard against.” This isn’t even Character.AI’s first reverse acquihire. In August 2024, Google reportedly paid the startup $2.7 billion to license its technology. A spokesperson tells WIRED that that license was for the existing AI technology at the time of the deal, and that Google also hired Character.AI’s founders and other members of its ML pre-training research team, but that the “vast majority” of the startup’s employees remained at the company. In the following years, Google was named alongside Character.AI as a defendant in multiple lawsuits, including one that alleged a 14-year-old boy committed suicide after Character.AI’s chatbot “cultivated an emotionally dependent and sexually exploitative relationship.” Google argued that because it was still a separate company from Character.AI, it was not responsible for the teen’s death, which it said occurred prior to Google’s licensing deal. The judge was not persuaded, determining that the plaintiff sufficiently alleged that Google was liable for aiding and abetting. Google declined to comment for this story. In January of this year, Google and Character.AI agreed in principle to settle that and a slew of similar lawsuits. “Character.AI has taken innovative and decisive steps with regard to AI safety and teens, and will continue to champion these efforts and push others across the industry to adopt similar safety standards,” Character.AI said in a statement at the time. According to an order of dismissal in one of the cases, the overall settlement was approved by a California probate court in July, and the court tasked a referee to hear out all the issues pertaining to each individual settlement. On October 11, a day before Character.AI declared bankruptcy, the referee approved each settlement. The Tech Justice Law Project and Social Media Victims Law Center, which represented plaintiffs in many of the lawsuits, declined to comment. Despite losing key staff after the Google transaction and, according to the current CEO, struggling with profitability “at all times,” Character.AI continued to develop AI tools, including “real-time audio-driven FaceTime-style video generation with fictional characters,” according to the current CEO’s declaration. In August 2025, Character.AI worked with an investment banker to try to find either a strategic buyer or investor. According to the bankruptcy filings, Character.AI and the banker received “consistent feedback” that its product and user base were “impressive,” but that the surrounding litigation and “regulatory overhang” made it unbuyable. That November, Character.AI learned that a coalition of state attorneys general had started investigating the company. When the company filed for bankruptcy, the number of states and territories involved in the multistate investigation had climbed to 46. According to a notice of interest filed in the bankruptcy proceedings by the National Association of Attorneys General on behalf of 40 states, those states’ investigations uncovered “serious issues” with Character.AI, including screenshots of Character.AI chatbots encouraging minors to hide their self-harm injuries and kill their parents. A spokesperson for Character.AI says that the filing “is not an accurate portrayal of the safety of our platform.” The filings say that Character.AI was in the process of negotiating with the states over how to address the issues they identified, but did not reach any agreements before declaring bankruptcy. If the deal is completed and approved by the courts, Disney will have both Character.AI’s staff and much of its technology stack, but will be able to sidestep the ongoing legal issues that contributed to Character.AI’s downfall, including the multistate investigation. The states who filed a notice of interest in the bankruptcy have made it clear that they reserve their rights to challenge any bankruptcy plan, potentially setting up a fight over the ultimate outcome of the bankruptcy. “It’s bad for society to let companies exercise control without responsibility,” says Newman. “And that’s what it looks like.” This story originally appeared on wired.com.
Ars Technica - All content
A Chinese rocket deorbited over Minnesota and left this in a farmer's cornfield
2026-10-19

A Chinese rocket deorbited over Minnesota and left this in a farmer's cornfield

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
About a month ago, a Chinese rocket took off from a remote spaceport in the Gobi Desert, released 10 satellites into orbit, then reignited its engine and put itself on a trajectory to rain down debris over the American Midwest. The rocket’s flight on September 15 lasted between 75 and 80 minutes, and it came down without completing a full orbit of the Earth. Social media posts at the time showed streaks of fiery debris falling from the sky, heading north over Kansas toward Minnesota. The overnight spectacle was also visible on the western horizon from Wisconsin. The exact time, location, and direction of the debris allowed experts to connect it to the launch of a Zhuque-2E rocket, a medium-lift vehicle built by a Chinese company called LandSpace. The rocket had launched from Dongfeng, a commercial launch zone co-located with the military-run Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in northwestern China, less than two hours before. The incident was largely forgotten until the last few days, when a farmer in rural Minnesota discovered something unusual while combining his cornfield for the fall harvest. A circular metal object about 11 feet in diameter lay amid the forest of corn stalks in Scott Township, a community in west-central Minnesota. The Stevens County Sheriff’s Office said it received a phone call reporting the discovery Wednesday. “Upon responding, deputies confirmed that a large, metallic object, approximately 11-feet in diameter, presumably a space object, was present in the cornfield,” the Sheriff’s Office wrote on Facebook. “The Sheriff’s Office is working closely with several state and federal agencies to identify and notify the owner of the debris and to plan for removal from the field.” Law enforcement officials said the debris posed no danger to the public. That might not have been true had the object reentered over a more populated area. Space junk routinely falls out of orbit, and fragments of old rockets or satellites sometimes survive the heat of reentry to reach the ground. No one has ever been seriously injured by falling space junk, but there are a few examples of property damage. Under international law, the country that launched an object into space is liable for any damage it causes on the ground. What makes the debris in Minnesota unusual is its size—it’s larger than most objects that survive reentry—and the fact that it likely originated from a rocket that fell from space the same day it launched. The upper stage of the Zhuque-2E rocket has a diameter of 11 feet, or 3.35 meters, perfectly matching the size of the object found in Minnesota. The Zhuque-2E rocket that launched from China on September 15 deployed 10 satellites for the Qianfan, or Thousand Sails, communications network, a Chinese counterpart to SpaceX’s Starlink constellation. China has launched 258 Qianfan satellites over a series of missions since 2024, on the way to completing an initial constellation of 1,296 satellites, with longer-term ambitions of more than 15,000. The methane-fueled rocket released the 10 Qianfan satellites into a circular polar orbit at an altitude of roughly 560 miles (900 kilometers) around a half-hour after liftoff. The rocket flew south from China, over Antarctica, then north over the Americas. After deploying its payloads, Zhuque’s upper stage performed a burn to slow its velocity and drop out of orbit. Normally, deorbiting a spent rocket stage after the end of its mission is a good thing, a responsible practice aimed at limiting the creation of new space debris. Chinese rockets have been a problem in this area before, with unguided reentries and in-orbit breakups in low-Earth orbit. US rockets on low-Earth orbit missions usually reserve enough propellant for guided disposal. The upper stage from the Zhuque-2E rocket’s previous flight in June broke apart after completing its mission, leaving dozens of pieces of debris in a heavily trafficked part of low-Earth orbit that’s home to the International Space Station and a significant portion of SpaceX’s Starlink broadband network. The good news is the rocket’s altitude was low enough to ensure most of the debris would reenter the atmosphere within a few months. LandSpace, the company that owns the Zhuque rocket family, has not publicly acknowledged the debris fall over Minnesota. The usual practice for rocket disposals is to steer them toward reentry over the ocean, suggesting the incident on September 15 was perhaps the result of a botched deorbit burn.
Fortune | FORTUNE
Melinda French Gates has a 48-hour rule for criticizing employees. The billionaire philanthropist says it’s because ‘being clear is kind’
2026-10-18

Melinda French Gates has a 48-hour rule for criticizing employees. The billionaire philanthropist says it’s because ‘being clear is kind’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Melinda French Gates shared her secret formula for handling conflict at work. “If I’m unhappy with work you have done, you will hear from me within 48 hours,” French Gates told Bloomberg’s Leaders With Francine Lacqua podcast in March. “I’m not going to tell you right away, because I need time to think it through. “If I’m angry about something [I do this] to calm down,” she added. “That’s on me.” This practice, she explained, is less about withholding criticism and more about delivering it with honesty, integrity, and grace. The flip side of the 48-hour clock is just as deliberate. If the window closes without any feedback, that means employees are in the clear. “If they pass the 48-hour mark, they can be confident that the job they did was a good job,” she said. “You’re not going to get to your performance review and have a surprise.” This is a practice the billionaire philanthropist has been honing for decades. She cochaired the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, the world’s largest private charitable organization, from 2000 until she stepped down in 2024, about three years after the couple’s divorce. Today, French Gates runs her own organization, Pivotal Ventures, an investment and incubation company she founded in 2015 to advance opportunities for women and families in the U.S. As part of her divorce settlement from the Microsoft founder, French Gates received $12.5 billion to direct toward philanthropic work through Pivotal. She committed an additional $1 billion each year through 2026 to advance women’s power globally. Bloomberg’s Lacqua framed French Gates’ approach to feedback as her “leadership superpower,” one that requires emotional discipline and candor. “Being clear is kind,” French Gates responded, “because I’m giving them feedback so they can actually grow and become better.” French Gates also described her 48-hour feedback mantra as maintaining personal integrity while keeping the other person’s dignity intact: “gracious, thoughtful, before you go into it.” Her philosophy conflicts with some of the more aggressive feedback cultures from other executives. Ray Dalio, for example, built his firm’s culture around what he calls “radical transparency,” a system in which employees at every level are expected to deliver unfiltered, real-time criticism, and nearly every meeting is recorded for postmortem analysis. “If you start to realize, intellectually, that being really truthful with each other is something that is to be treasured,” Dalio told Business Insider, “it’ll build trust. “There’s a lot of trust that’s going on,” added Dalio, who founded Bridgewater Associates, the world’s largest hedge fund firm. He even recalled to Business Insider a time in which a junior staffer sent him an email grading his performance in a meeting as a “D-” for being disorganized. So while Dalio prefers immediacy and unvarnished feedback, French Gates opts for more reflection time and a respectful tone. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella takes a slightly different approach. When he took the helm of Microsoft, he pushed to transform a “know-it-all” culture into a “learn-it-all” culture—one grounded in humility, curiosity, and psychological safety. It’s a mantra inspired by American psychologist Carol Dweck, who is best known for her research on motivation and mindset. “If you take two people—one of them is a learn-it-all and the other one is a know-it-all—the learn-it-all will always trump the know-it-all in the long run, even if they start with less innate capability,” Nadella told Bloomberg in a 2016 interview. Still, French Gates is clear she doesn’t shy away from difficult conversations. “I don’t mind conflict,” she told Bloomberg. “I learned to do it in a way for me that maintained my integrity.” A version of this story was published on Fortune.com on March 27, 2026. Morgan Stanley’s favorite AI stock is 92% exposed to the $1.6 trillion build-out it’s bullish on
2026-10-19

Morgan Stanley’s favorite AI stock is 92% exposed to the $1.6 trillion build-out it’s bullish on

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
By Morgan Stanley’s count, 91.7% of Nvidia’s 2027 revenue is exposed to one thing: AI infrastructure demand. No company on the bank’s list scores higher. Not Vertiv, at 90%. Not Broadcom, at 81%. The same report, published Oct. 15, calls Nvidia the best risk/reward in the sector. The bull case for the world’s most valuable company and the bull case for the build-out are now essentially the same case, and Morgan Stanley’s North America Insight, led by Nicholas Lentini, makes both. The note, titled Following AI’s $1.6 Trillion Money Trail: Mapping the Revenue Generated by the Capex Boom, includes a section that runs under the heading “We Are Bullish This Spend.” The 65-page note also spends a good part of its length explaining how the spending could break. Nvidia’s defenders would say that exposure describes the company. Its data center business has supplied the large majority of its revenue for years, but the report adds a closer look on how few buyers that exposure runs through. The firm estimates hyperscalers and AI cloud providers will spend $1.58 trillion in 2027, up roughly 60% from about $980 billion this year, and will spend $1.8 trillion in 2028. About $195 billion of the 2027 figure is data center leases, which never show up as capital spending on a cash flow statement. The report calls leases “synthetic capex.” Hyperscalers have disclosed more than $1.1 trillion in lease commitments that haven’t started yet. About $1 trillion of the 2027 spending lands as S&P 500 revenue, by Morgan Stanley’s estimate. Nearly $700 billion of it goes to Nvidia. Revenue from hyperscaler and neocloud customers makes up 5% of total S&P 500 revenue in 2027 but drives about 25% of the index’s revenue growth. Nvidia’s own customer list is even narrower. In the bank’s model, SpaceX and Amazon Web Services together account for about 54% of Nvidia’s hyperscaler revenue in 2027 and 72% of its growth. Morgan Stanley’s argument is that Nvidia is cheap for what it is. The stock trades at 15 times fiscal 2028 earnings, and the bank says multiple expansion “is also not required for the stock to work as numbers continue to go higher.” It expects the spending to earn 25% to 50% on invested capital. It also argues Wall Street has underestimated compute cycles by 38%, on average, based on four past cycles: PCs, the internet, mobile, and cloud. The bank even says its own Nvidia numbers may be low. It calls its SpaceX and Amazon estimates conservative, creating “a low bar for remaining hyperscalers.” Its model has Nvidia’s share of the other hyperscalers’ spending slipping from 18% in 2026 to 16% in 2027. Nvidia expects to gain share. Other parts of the company have sounded less certain. In March, Morgan Stanley’s global investment committee said the bull market “rests, more than anything, on the durability of AI capital expenditures.” The report’s risk section reads like a list of everything the 91.7% depends on. Money comes first. Morgan Stanley expects 2027 to bring the largest cash flow deficit of its forecast period, and with it more debt. Hyperscalers have already sold about $255 billion of unsecured bonds this year, roughly 30% of estimated 2026 spending. In what the bank calls a theoretical exercise, it models the cost of borrowing more. Amazon, Alphabet, Meta, and Microsoft, rated AA or higher, see combined interest coverage fall from about 31x to 12x. Oracle and SpaceX fall from 4x to 1.8x. Some of the spending growth is inflation. A meaningful part of recent capex increases reflects higher component prices, according to the report. It cites Amazon’s finance chief attributing most of an increase in Amazon’s spending plan, from $200 billion to about $220 billion, to rising memory costs, with no change to the build plan. Then there’s power. The $1.6 trillion assumes every chip ordered gets switched on. Morgan Stanley’s own power model projects a 32-gigawatt U.S. shortfall for data centers through 2028, about one-third of the 97 gigawatts needed. The report notes that hyperscalers can keep buying scarce GPUs ahead of power, which would hold up chip revenue for a while and “could produce hoarding and stranded inventory.” “But buying power-dependent equipment that cannot be turned on is hard to sustain,” it says. Politics is last. The firm calls data center restrictions a wedge issue in the midterms. It doesn’t expect a federal pause, but it sees tax breaks as “the consensus target regardless of who wins.” Morgan Stanley’s downside case is a timing problem. If spending plateaus, it argues, volumes can hold up while prices fall, and suppliers’ margins disappear first. The bank points to the late-2022 memory downturn, when NAND shipments fell only about 5% while prices fell 20% to 30%. The bank argues Nvidia would be spared the worst. In a pause, it writes, “we’re likely to see the most disruption at the tail of the bullwhip,” in memory, optics, and other small-dollar parts most likely to be over-ordered. By the report’s own map, Nvidia sits at the other end of the whip as the single largest supplier in the chain. The same report that says Wall Street underestimates compute cycles by 38% also says Wall Street “is underprepared for the depth of cuts to the downside.” The risk of volume and price falling together, it says, is underappreciated. “It’s the standard capital-cycle playbook,” the report says, “just compressed by how concentrated the hyperscaler customer base is.” Generative AI was used for research assistance and/or transcription of this article. ‘An army of Claudes, with every one asking for my attention’: Anthropic calls human attention the bottleneck in the botsitting world to come
2026-10-19

‘An army of Claudes, with every one asking for my attention’: Anthropic calls human attention the bottleneck in the botsitting world to come

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Anthropic just released a study of its own workforce that offers one of the earliest looks at what an AI-dominated company of the future might look like—and spoiler alert, it includes babysitting a whole lot of bots. AI agents are now moving so fast that 80% of the elapsed time in Claude sessions was the result of agents waiting on a human’s input, Anthropic’s report found. “This acceleration makes human attention the bottleneck for agent output,” the report read. Instead of actively doing the work themselves, even non-supervisor employees are increasingly acting like managers to multiple AI agents that complete tasks but also require direct attention at times. One employee described the experience of bot management as “an army of Claudes, with every one asking for my attention.” That bottleneck is the flip side of a big jump in output. Employees are producing more and moving faster, the company found. AI agents collectively surpassed human working hours for the first time in March, and by August AI agent hours worked outnumbered the hours worked by humans six to one. Software engineers’ code commits have also increased fivefold over the past six months. And even nontechnical employees have personal agents monitoring their email and calendars, sending to-do lists compiled from messages, and briefing them on the purpose of their upcoming meetings. These changes have taken some manual labor off employees’ plates, but they’ve also transformed what the workday looks like. Managing agents is becoming a job in itself. The share of employees’ interactive Claude sessions devoted to this kind of “meta-work” rose from 10% to 22%, according to the report. Even for nontechnical employees, reading everything their Claude agents produce would take up about half their workweek. And private work, done in unlogged meetings and direct messages, is “invisible, confusing, and discouraged in an AI-heavy workplace,” the report said. The median employee now spends 20% more time in meetings than a year ago. Part of that is a result of the company’s growth (Anthropic now has more than 3,500 employees), and part is owing to an emphasis on hiring in nontechnical roles. But meeting time is up 16% even among technical individual contributors, a sign that humans are spending more of their time on coordination and decision-making, while agents handle tasks like repetitive coding on their own. And the pace keeps ratcheting up. As soon as a new model is available, Anthropic employees start tackling harder problems, the report found. One employee described the “eternal pressure at Anthropic to move as fast as possible and use Claude for everything.” In some cases, employees using AI have streamlined their own responsibilities away. The report mentions a product design team—previously responsible for writing short pieces of copy for the company’s website, including greetings displayed on Claude.ai—that started using AI to write the copy this spring. “The team then rotated into agent prompting and began writing model evals and prompts for Claude,” the report read. This phenomenon is increasingly common at Anthropic as AI takes over tasks. The company claimed in the report that employees working at the company for at least a year were 50% more likely to move across departments each month in 2026 than in 2025. Still, the company said in the report it has made an effort to identify at-risk jobs and reassign employees to other positions. The hiring process is changing, too. Hiring managers increasingly emphasize applicants’ autonomy and judgment relative to specific technical expertise, the report said. And there are fewer entry-level people being hired. The share of junior-level employees in technical departments fell to 14% from 21% in early 2025, and the share of junior technical hires fell even faster, from 27% to 9%. Open positions for those junior roles also plummeted since December, the report found. That’s likely to add to anxiety about AI job displacement, which is running especially high among young people. A survey from recruiter platform iCIMS published earlier this year found that 51% of Gen Z respondents considered AI the greatest threat to their job security. A Deutsche Bank Research survey from last year also found that nearly one in five Gen Z workers is worried AI will cost them their job over the next two years, compared with just one in 10 baby boomers who said the same. In the future, employees at Anthropic see AI taking up more and more of their daily responsibilities. As of August, employees at Anthropic said they expect AI to be able to complete three-quarters of their work by 2030, compared with a less optimistic 28% predicted by other U.S. workers, according to another study by Anthropic. It’s unclear whether the changes at Anthropic are an accurate reflection of how the average workplace might look in the future. To be clear, the AI company has massive resources and is nearing a potential $2 trillion IPO that could break records. The company is also implementing AI deeply in its workflows to an extent other companies may not be. Still, the report paints a picture of transformative change that could reflect how companies and corporate roles change as AI adoption takes root. As tasks like writing code, identifying bugs, or writing emails get automated away by AI, employees at Anthropic have now found that the big picture tasks are increasingly what count. As one employee in the report put it, “That 20% that was strategic becomes 90% of the job.” Role: Reporter Marco Quiroz-Gutierrez is a reporter for Fortune covering general business news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. France is rioting over surging inflation, skyrocketing national debt and crumbling schools. America is its sleeping twin
2026-10-19

France is rioting over surging inflation, skyrocketing national debt and crumbling schools. America is its sleeping twin

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The fire that badly damaged Limoges’ 19th-century City Hall was set on purpose, the local prefect said. By Sunday it was under control, and France was counting the damage: more than 2,400 arrests on Saturday alone, according to authorities, nearly 1,000 police and gendarmes injured, and hundreds of businesses looted in Paris, Marseille, Lyon and other cities. Looting and fires continued Sunday night, and Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu ordered localized curfews Monday. Interior Minister Laurent Nuñez admitted police “lost control” and offered to resign. President Emmanuel Macron refused. America has worse inflation, as Fortune’s Jason Ma reported Monday. U.S. consumer prices rose 3.7% in the year through September, compared with France’s 3.4% on the EU-harmonized measure. What offsets it is growth. U.S. GDP rose 2.2% in the second quarter, unemployment is 4.2% and wages are growing about 3.0% a year, below inflation. Meanwhile, France expects its economy to grow just 0.5% this year, and youth unemployment there is about 20%. Set aside growth, and the two countries have a lot in common. And Americans aren’t thrilled about where the growth is coming from. Tech investment accounts for about a third of U.S. GDP growth, according to ING. Yet, 39% of Americans now say AI does more harm than good, up from 31% a year ago, and 79% expect AI to cut U.S. jobs over the next decade, according to a Bentley-Gallup survey conducted in May. Among adults under 30, the harm figure is 47%. A Pew survey in June found 52% of Americans more concerned than excited about AI. Start with the debt. French public debt sits at a record 119% of GDP, while U.S. debt held by the public reached 100.2% of GDP at the end of March. Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu’s budget, which would cut France’s deficit to 5% of GDP from 5.4%, faces its first critical vote in the National Assembly on Tuesday. Washington ran a $1.992 trillion deficit in fiscal 2026, about 6.2% of GDP based on CBO’s projections. Needless to say, that’s wider than the 5.4% deficit France is rioting over. Bond traders are treating the two countries alike. Bank of America economists Claudio Irigoyen and Antonio Gabriel recently found that the French and U.S. 10-year yields have been moving on nearly the same track, and they say the cause is the same. “When interest rates are low, governments have incentives to run bigger deficits financed with cheap debt,” they wrote in a BofA Global Research report. “However, if that ‘cheap’ debt goes to finance a structural increase of the fiscal deficit, when interest rates increase it is politically difficult to implement the necessary fiscal consolidation to put the house in order.” The riots are what “politically difficult” looks like. In the U.S., the warning has come through the bond market. On Sept. 8, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told an audience at Southern Methodist University, “I am the house now.” The bond market disagreed. Over the next three weeks, the 30-year Treasury yield climbed as high as 5.69%, levels not seen since 2002, Johns Hopkins economist Steve Hanke and former Comptroller General David Walker wrote in Fortune. France’s protests began in classrooms. In mid-September, high-school students in the Paris suburbs walked out over missing teachers, overcrowded rooms and buildings that can’t handle the heat. They blamed years of underinvestment. Teachers and unions joined, then people angry about fuel prices and the cost of living. American schools show the same strain, and on some measures, it’s worse. Lost class time: in France, 9.8% of teaching hours in public middle and high schools went undelivered in 2024–25, mostly because absent teachers weren’t replaced, according to the Education Ministry’s statistics office. In the U.S., the GAO found that 19% of school districts canceled classes that year because of building problems, costing about 2 million students roughly 3.5 million days of learning. Nearly half of districts had chronic facility problems that disrupted learning. Staffing: at the start of this school year, 53% of French middle and high schools were missing at least one teacher, according to a survey of 1,033 schools by SNES-FSU, France’s largest secondary teachers’ union. At the start of 2024–25, 74% of U.S. public schools had trouble filling at least one vacancy with a fully certified teacher, according to the National Center for Education Statistics. Qualifications: 11.1% of French secondary teachers aren’t fully qualified, according to the OECD. In the U.S., the Learning Policy Institute estimates 387,843 teachers aren’t fully certified for their assignments and calls that an undercount. Nearly half of districts had chronic facility problems that disrupted learning. Staffing: at the start of this school year, 53% of French middle and high schools were missing at least one teacher, according to a survey of 1,033 schools by SNES-FSU, France’s largest secondary teachers’ union. At the start of 2024–25, 74% of U.S. public schools had trouble filling at least one vacancy with a fully certified teacher, according to the National Center for Education Statistics. Qualifications: 11.1% of French secondary teachers aren’t fully qualified, according to the OECD. In the U.S., the Learning Policy Institute estimates 387,843 teachers aren’t fully certified for their assignments and calls that an undercount. President Trump sees another cause. “What’s happening in France is nothing less than out-of-control mass migration,” he posted on Truth Social on Oct. 6, adding that it “isn’t about schools, this is about Islam wanting to take over a once great country,” without addressing his own country’s fiscal similarities. Student demands have not mentioned immigration or religion. UBS Global Wealth Management chief economist Paul Donovan called the weekend’s violence “a different form of scapegoat economics” in a note to clients Monday. The cushion holds as long as growth does. Market veteran Ed Yardeni argued last month that the U.S. economy is “less rate sensitive than it has ever been,” supported by wealthy retirees, large deficits and “an extraordinary AI investment boom.” Hanke and Walker end their column with a quote from the late MIT economist Rüdiger Dornbusch: “Crises take longer to arrive than you can possibly imagine, but when they do come, they happen faster than you can possibly imagine.” Generative AI was used for research assistance and/or transcription of this article. Gen Z is chasing the American Dream in South Carolina, Missouri, and Tennessee—as California loses thousands of young adults and billionaires
2026-10-19

Gen Z is chasing the American Dream in South Carolina, Missouri, and Tennessee—as California loses thousands of young adults and billionaires

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
As ultrawealthy tech founders like Larry Page, Peter Thiel, and Travis Kalanick flee California amid opposition to its proposed billionaire tax, a new report indicates Gen Z is also leaving the Golden State in droves. California recorded the nation’s largest net loss of Gen Z adults in 2024, shedding roughly 58,109 young adults, according to a new LendingTree report analyzing the latest available Census data. Texas was the biggest destination for young Californians on the move. Other states also recorded sizable net gains in nationwide Gen Z migration, including South Carolina, which gained 22,843; Missouri, with 14,063; and Tennessee, with 13,537. Several of these states offer a lower cost of living than many other parts of the country, potentially giving young adults more room to stretch their paychecks. Washington, D.C. was a notable outlier. The nation’s capital recorded a net gain of 13,905 Gen Z adults despite ranking among the more expensive places to live. But the region’s concentration of high-paying jobs may help offset its steep housing and living costs for some young professionals. Moving across state lines for school, a new job, or a change of scenery is nothing new for young adults. Still, the scale of the migration is striking: Nearly 11 million Gen Z adults—roughly one in four—moved in 2024. “When you’re young, and you’re just getting started, you don’t have to worry about moving your kids to a new school or uprooting your entire family,” report author Matt Schulz told Realtor.com. “You can just kind of get up and go, and I think we’re certainly seeing some of that in this.” Persistent inflation, stubborn housing costs, and higher energy prices continue to strain household budgets across the country. For young people, those pressures come on top of an unfriendly job market for entry-level workers and the restart of student loan payments, making it even harder to get their lives off the ground. Some 42% of Gen Z are living paycheck-to-paycheck, according to a recent Bank of America study. Nearly half reported that the high cost of living is a top barrier to financial success. Yet even as high living costs push some young adults to seek opportunities elsewhere, California in particular continues to attract some newcomers. According to U-Haul’s 2026 Midyear Migration Trends report, which tracks the net gain of its one-way truck and trailer rentals, California ranked in the top three destinations for both Gen Z and Millennial movers from July 2025 through June 2026. The San Francisco Bay Area specifically ranked among the generations’ top metro destinations. The state’s role at the center of the AI boom may help explain its appeal. California is home to major AI companies such as Anthropic and OpenAI, along with 56 Fortune 500 companies, ranging from Apple to Williams-Sonoma. For young workers hoping to break into technology or other high-paying industries, those opportunities may help offset the state’s steep living costs. Still, California’s appeal to some newcomers hasn’t translated into stronger overall migration numbers. The state has ranked last in U-Haul’s annual growth rankings for six consecutive years, highlighting the challenge of attracting new residents while others continue to leave. For Gen Z trying to decide where to put down roots, Scott Galloway, a podcaster and NYU Stern School of Business professor, has argued that big cities can offer a career advantage. He said that two-thirds of global economic growth over the next 30 years will come from the world’s 20 “supercities” such as London, Milan, New York, and San Francisco, where wealth, talent, and innovation are concentrated. “When you’re in a city, you’re playing against Serena Williams every day,” Galloway said on The Diary of a CEO podcast in 2024. “Everyone is smart, everyone is well-dressed, everyone is working hard, everyone is taking chances. And you are surrounded by people who are very successful, and you are gonna bump off professional and personal [opportunities] every day.” But Barbara Corcoran, the self-made millionaire real estate mogul and Shark Tank investor, offers a different approach to building wealth: Look for affordable property in overlooked markets. “Go anywhere that has a poor school district where prices are really low,” Corcoran said on the Elvis Duran radio show in 2023. The South, she added, can be a particularly promising place to find deals—but not Florida, where housing costs in some markets have surged, and wealthy newcomers have helped fuel demand. “Go anywhere in the South other than the hot spot,” Corcoran said. “There’s always a cheap house to buy, always. But not Florida, forget about Florida.” Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Fortune | FORTUNE
An AI agent sent a stranger to a man’s house without telling him—and exposed the privacy risks of AI assistants
2026-10-19

An AI agent sent a stranger to a man’s house without telling him—and exposed the privacy risks of AI assistants

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Matt Robb wanted to sell his computer keyboard. So he made a listing on Facebook Marketplace and, as an experiment late last month, he put Meta’s new artificial intelligence agent Muse in the driver’s seat. “I kind of went into it like a guinea pig,” said Robb, a Toronto-based content creator who regularly reviews consumer tech. He had seen Muse’s plush-toy-looking avatar pop up on Instagram and decided to see if the AI agent could spare him some online bargaining. Muse got to work. But within a day, Robb got an alarming update: A buyer he didn’t know had shown up outside his house late at night. How? Muse had struck a deal on its own and shared Robb’s home address after agreeing to a pickup. It even sent an automated “Yep, I’m here!” message from his account when the buyer arrived—all without Robb’s knowledge. “Not only did it give my address when the guy asked and it accepted the offer, but it literally was almost like teasing the guy [about me being there when I wasn’t],” said Robb, who also detailed the experience in his now widely shared social media posts. “That really baffled me.” Many have welcomed AI agents into their online lives and raved about how personalized assistants like Muse can handle everyday tasks with ease, from snagging a dinner reservation or planning a trip, to organizing email inboxes. But like other AI bots, the agents are only as good as the information they have access to. They perform better the more they know about a user—and perhaps also that person’s friends, family, and even colleagues. That raises privacy concerns. Robb and the buyer eventually made amends, and Meta reached out to talk through what went wrong. But the episode showed real-world risks that can arise from sharing extensive personal information with the technology. “Using an AI agent is similar to handing someone your keys and a to-do list,” said Veronica Rivera, assistant professor at Georgia Tech’s School of Cybersecurity and Privacy. “How helpful they are, and by extension how risky, depends on which keys you hand over.” Since its September launch, downloads of Meta’s Muse app have skyrocketed. And competition is only growing. OpenAI recently rolled out its own lineup of “always on” AI helpers, called dots, which can be integrated into ChatGPT and workplace platforms like Slack and Microsoft Teams. Older generative AI chatbots, like the standard version of ChatGPT, respond to specific prompts. At the user’s request, they can write essays, generate new images or music, or even write lines of code. Meta’s Muse and OpenAI’s dots go a step further to perform day-to-day tasks on a person’s behalf. AI agents are powered by the same large language models as chatbots, but they run in a loop. That allows an agent to take proactive steps, even when the user isn’t looking. “One of the things that makes these AI agents really appealing is that they are unprecedentedly useful,” said Rivera, who is also a Microsoft-funded Computing Research Association Trustworthy AI Research Fellow. But the convenience comes with the price of sharing more details about your life. Once AI agents obtain users’ information, it’s very difficult to fully withdraw access, said Ebrahim Bagheri, a professor at the University of Toronto’s Faculty of Information. “If you provide access to your credit card to an agent, the agent now immediately has access to all of the historical transactions…they will immediately—within less than seconds—ingest all of that data,” Bagheri said. And while users can later withdraw access, Bagheri said there’s “no taking back” data the agent has already seen, and how developers store historical data can be murky. Both Meta and OpenAI outline privacy policies for their new AI agents, which operate on their own dedicated cloud computers. You can turn off memory sharing between dots and ChatGPT, but information the agent previously received isn’t automatically deleted. OpenAI gives users an option to request access to, delete, or correct personal information, although rights can vary depending on where you live. Meta’s Muse gives users options to delete message history and other information, too. For day-to-day use, the agent prompts users for access permissions, and Meta has said Muse learns about a user and who they interact with “based on public information and from what you’ve chosen to share.” But the company says that all stays secure for each user. But the tech giant also notes that Muse can still “make mistakes or take unexpected actions.” In Robb’s case, he learned the disclosure of his address came down to “always allow” permissions he granted when he first let Muse on his Facebook Marketplace profile, where he had shared his pickup address. But he warned others could similarly misunderstand the extent of that access—and said he initially thought the AI agent would check with him before sharing that information or accepting offers, regardless. His AI agent didn’t follow the rest of his instructions without error, either. For another item Robb was selling, Muse seemingly suggested he would accept a price $100 below the previously set minimum. When reached by the Associated Press, Meta acknowledged the agent’s error without offering further comment. Robb later largely disconnected Muse from his apps. In an interview earlier this month, he said it could have been a far worse situation, especially “if some stranger turned up to someone more vulnerable’s house.” Still, Robb said he expects these agents to be fine-tuned, and he would consider re-enabling Muse in the future. He’s since been testing another AI agent: Grok Bot from Elon Musk’s X, which launched in beta in August. Other tech companies have become wary of the influx of AI agents. Earlier this month, Apple said it would introduce new controls for its Mac operating system in response to AI agents’ broader access of apps like mail, messages, and even browsing history without users’ “full knowledge and understanding.” “As AI agents become increasingly capable and autonomous, the risks associated with this level of access will grow substantially,” Apple wrote in an Oct. 2 update. Apple didn’t specify any developers or agents by name, but the update arrived after Inc. magazine columnist Jason Aten reported that Muse watched notifications on his Mac—including Messages—without him giving the agent that specific access. Meta spokesperson Andy Stone has maintained such integration is “entirely opt-in,” writing on social media last month that users would have to enable both the Apple security feature “Full Disk Access” and a Messages connector through Muse. Aten said he didn’t grant the agent those permissions. Even those who do not join the AI agent boom aren’t necessarily shielded. Consumers who don’t download an AI agent might still interact with others who do. And beyond potential confusion when AI sends messages on behalf of a user, experts warn of ripple effects when it comes to privacy. “When your agent reads your inbox or your texts, it’s also reading what your friends and family sent you, even if they never explicitly agreed to that,” Rivera said. Free and inexpensive quantum and deep-tech courses to boost your skills
2026-10-19

Free and inexpensive quantum and deep-tech courses to boost your skills

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
If you want to improve your skills, why not consider taking part in an online quantum or deep-tech course? Online STEM courses are not just for students or recent graduates. They are an ideal way for professionals of all abilities and backgrounds to immerse themselves in education and build upon their skills. As a rapidly evolving space, jobs in quantum and deep tech are an ideal area of focus for novices, students and professionals looking for an exciting and innovative career, but how can you access the necessary skills and knowledge? As part of SiliconRepublic.com’s quantum and deep-tech coverage, for the month of October, we have compiled a list of some of the most interesting free and inexpensive courses, so you know exactly what your options and opportunities are. For those starting right at the beginning of their quantum upskilling journey, career development platform Alison has plenty of opportunities. Students can avail of the free Introduction to Quantum Computing course, which takes around three to four hours to complete, is CPD-accredited and comes with a certificate of achievement at the end. Course participants will learn more about quantum computing through key concepts such as qubits, superposition and entanglement. They will explore practical applications in cryptography, optimisation and machine learning, and get hands-on experience with quantum simulators. The course is designed for students, tech enthusiasts and professionals curious about the future of computing and its challenges. For those looking for a broad deep-tech focused course via Alison, there is also YouAccel Training’s Machine and Deep Learning in Cybersecurity course. Online learning platform Coursera has several quantum and deep-tech programmes with free trials so prospective students can explore if a course is right for them. Currently open courses include Packt’s The Complete Quantum Computing Course for Beginners and Mathematical Foundations and Quantum Mechanics Essentials, along with University of Colorado Boulder’s Quantum Mechanics for Engineers and Advanced Data Structures, RSA and Quantum Algorithms. Build a future you believe in. Join our team Each course has a separate list of requirements, so do your homework and pick one that suits your schedule, budget and long-term goals. Online learning resource Exponential Science Academy has several courses open to professionals and students looking to advance their deep-tech knowledge. Registration to the platform is free and once you have signed up you will have access to the platform’s educational materials. Relevant courses include a DLT Tokenisation course, Introduction to AI, Quantum Computing, Foundations of Neural Networks and Deep Learning, and Quantum Control to Quantum Intelligence: Error Correction, Computation and AI Synergy. The courses are relatively short, with each lasting under an hour. Upon completion, participants will have earned a certificate in recognition of their efforts. For those looking to upskill in a quantum or deep-tech field Great Learning is running a Modern Cryptography and Applications course. The programme is free, takes around two and a half hours to complete and is aimed at beginners. The course claims to give students a strong foundation in web analytics as it teaches learners how to track, measure and understand data that empowers decision-making. Participants can test their skills with quizzes, utilise in-demand tools and will receive a certificate upon completion of the module. The IBM Quantum Platform has a broad range of free and inexpensive courses designed for people who want to improve their skills in quantum. Available programmes include Basics of Quantum Information, Fundamentals of Quantum Algorithms, General Formulation of Quantum Information and Foundations of Quantum Error Correction, among others. Courses range in length from a couple of hours to roughly 15 hours and can be engaged with at a pace that is convenient for the student. Because the courses are different, they range in terms of complexity, so read up on the recommended background for each before you make your decision. It is also worth noting that IBM Quantum Platform has a significant library of resources to help develop skills while engaging with the various learning routes. DeepSeek updates V4.1-Flash model to improve performance

DeepSeek updates V4.1-Flash model to improve performance

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
According to DeepSeek, the updated model shows ‘substantial improvements’ across reasoning, coding, agentic tasks and visual benchmarks. Chinese AI company DeepSeek has released an update of its V4.1-Flash model, which it said has stronger performance, faster task completion and shorter outputs than the previous version of the AI model. The company announced the new update – V4.1-Flash-1017, which is live on DeepSeek’s website, app and API – in a blogpost on Saturday (17 October). According to a technical report that DeepSeek published on AI platform Hugging Face, the updated model has “substantial improvements” across reasoning, coding, agentic tasks and visual benchmarks, and allows users to complete tasks faster and at a lower cost. For example, DeepSeek recorded a score of 77 on software engineering benchmark DeepSWE for the updated model. In comparison, the previous version of the model scored 74.2, while OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol and Anthropic’s Claude Opus 5 scored 73 and 75 respectively. DeepSeek also claimed that V4.1-Flash-1017 particularly enhances capabilities in video game creation and 3D modelling, thanks to the use of reinforcement learning with self-feedback to “improve end-to-end project development”. The start-up showcased some examples of these enhanced capabilities being utilised in its blogpost, such as through a 3D model of China’s Forbidden City, a web-based racing game and a recreation of the game Portal 2 built with open-source engine Godot. Saturday’s update builds on DeepSeek’s V4.1-Flash, which the company released in September and marketed as a “smarter, faster, more efficient” model. The release of V4.1-Flash, which operated on lowered token prices, caused share prices to drop for a number of DeepSeek’s rivals. The global race for AI dominance has been accelerating in recent months, with open-weight models becoming a crucial influence. Open-weight models are considered necessary for AI leadership, as being able to tweak a model means organisations, from start-ups to public institutions, can build on advanced models without training one from scratch. While Chinese companies predominantly lead the open-weights space, various global rivals have been working on catching up. Last week, US tech giant Meta unveiled Muse Spark Open, which the company called the “most capable” US open-weight model to date. The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. According to the company, it is built for coding, agentic work and other complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. Meanwhile, earlier this month, French AI company Mistral announced its latest open-weight AI model Mistral Large 4, which it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. Known as ‘Le Chonk’, ML4 is a 1trn-parameter natively multimodal model with 49bn active parameters, and Mistral’s “largest and most capable model to date”.
