I Faced Huge Hurdles to Get Veteran Benefits. The Supreme Court Has a Chance to Make Things Better.
2026-10-19

I Faced Huge Hurdles to Get Veteran Benefits. The Supreme Court Has a Chance to Make Things Better.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. While stationed in Cold War–era Germany, Army infantryman Floyd Johnson experienced a training exercise that turned deadly. Decades later, while incarcerated in Florida, he was finally diagnosed with service-connected post-traumatic stress disorder from his experience. The Department of Veterans Affairs assigned him a 70 percent disability rating, later increasing it to 80 percent on appeal. However, because Johnson is incarcerated, the VA only pays his disability compensation at a 10 percent rate under a federal law governing benefits for incarcerated veterans. Johnson is challenging that law as unconstitutional. But first, the U.S. Supreme Court has to decide whether a district court will hear the case at all. At oral argument earlier this month, the government argued that it cannot. It said instead that Johnson must first pursue his claim through the VA’s administrative review process and then seek review in the Court of Appeals for Veterans Claims. This process can take years and requires veterans to navigate multiple layers of review before reaching a court, which is problematic enough. Worse though, the conventional understanding is that VA administrative bodies cannot declare an act of Congress unconstitutional, so Johnson would have to spend years in waiting for guaranteed administrative denials before ever setting foot in a court. The dispute arises from the Veterans’ Judicial Review Act of 1988 and the Supreme Court’s decision in Johnson v. Robison. In 1974, the court held that an earlier statute restricting judicial review of veterans’ benefits decisions did not prevent federal district courts from hearing constitutional challenges to a law governing veterans’ benefits. In response, Congress established the current system, routing most disputes through the VA and its specialized appellate courts. Johnson argues that in doing so, Congress did not eliminate district court jurisdiction over constitutional challenges to the statutes themselves. There are good reasons for specialized review systems. Veterans’ benefits law is governed by a complex constellation of federal statutes and regulations, and adjudicating claims often requires expertise in military records, medical evidence, and the connection between military service and disability. Congress established the current system to promote consistency and provide veterans with a dedicated process for challenging adverse decisions, but the benefits of specialized review are difficult to defend when a claimant must navigate years of administrative proceedings before a court can even consider resolving the legal question at the heart of the dispute. I know how much that delay can cost. I left the Army in 2009 with a general discharge under honorable conditions after behavior related to PTSD from my time in Iraq. Like it did Johnson, the government told me that I needed to go through a time-consuming administrative process with little chance of success before I could bring the case to a district court. About six years into trying to navigate that process with no success, by chance, I mentioned my case to the clinical professor in charge of the Veterans Legal Services Clinic at Yale Law School. The clinic took up my claim, bringing a class-action lawsuit challenging the Army’s handling of discharge upgrade applications. Even then, it took a small army of law students, professors, and pro bono attorneys, along with the direct intervention and oversight of the district court, for me to receive my honorable discharge and access to desperately needed benefits—nine years after being discharged with severe PTSD. As in Johnson’s case, the government moved to dismiss my lawsuit in part because I had not exhausted my administrative remedies. But my claim was that the administrative process itself was broken. The Army Discharge Review Board had repeatedly denied my applications without considering legal guidance from the Secretary of Defense to give liberal consideration to veterans whose service-connected mental health conditions contributed to their misconduct. I was told to keep pursuing the very process that I knew was not being conducted in accordance with the law. Fortunately, the judge disagreed with that demand. He rejected the government’s motion to dismiss and agreed to hear the case in district court. In response, the Army granted my discharge upgrade with no additional evidence and then moved to dismiss the class action because I had already been upgraded, which would have allowed it to continue illegally ignoring the secretary’s guidance. The judge again rejected the motion to dismiss, and then, only under the threat of further court intervention, the Army agreed to settle and allow new reviews for almost 60,000 veterans, now following the proper guidelines and giving liberal consideration to the impact of mental health conditions in each veteran’s conduct. The Army denied wrongdoing, but the settlement required it to reconsider cases and change procedures. Despite the Army’s earlier protestations, the sky didn’t fall when the court was allowed to get involved. Veterans just got another chance to have their cases fairly heard. Johnson’s case raises a different legal question since he is challenging the constitutionality of a statute rather than the administration of an existing policy, but my experience shows why courts should be wary of rules that require veterans to exhaust administrative remedies even when they allege that the system itself is failing them. Specialized processes can offer expertise and consistency, but they should not become barriers to judicial review when the costs of delay are high and the claimant’s legal argument cannot possibly be resolved through ordinary administrative decision making. The Supreme Court doesn’t need to dismantle the veterans’ review system to recognize that some cases warrant a more direct route to federal court. Johnson’s case asks whether Congress intended to foreclose district court review of constitutional challenges to veterans’ benefits laws. My case showed what can happen when the government insists that veterans keep navigating an administrative process even when that is the very system that they take legal issue with. Specialized review can provide expertise and consistency, but those benefits cannot justify making access to justice contingent on years of procedural hurdles. During my lawsuit, I often found myself wondering how many veterans had died waiting for benefits that might have saved them. That thought still haunts me. No court can give someone back years spent without healthcare, housing, or the education benefits they earned through military service and desperately need. When the government’s own procedures will not, or cannot, deliver the relief the law promises—or as in Johnson’s case, a potentially unconstitutional law cannot provide—veterans need somewhere else to turn. Access to an independent court cannot be a prize reserved for those lucky enough to find a lawyer and persistent enough to survive years of delay. It is a necessary safeguard against a government that fails to keep its promises to the people who served it. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Yes, Congress Can Charge Donald Trump With Murder
2026-10-19

Yes, Congress Can Charge Donald Trump With Murder

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. This is the fourth in a series of articles, “Try, Try Again?,” drawn from my new book—Great and Dangerous Offences: The Case for (and Against) Impeaching Trump a Third Time—due out in early 2027. At the beginning of his national political career, Donald Trump famously said that he could shoot someone on Fifth Avenue in New York and not lose political support. In her dissent from the 2024 Supreme Court opinion granting Trump and his presidential successors unprecedented immunity for crimes committed while in office, Justice Sonia Sotomayor observed incredulously that should a president order “the Navy’s Seal Team 6 to assassinate a political rival,” he would nonetheless be “immune” from criminal prosecution. The first statement may remain an accurate description of the devotion of Trump’s most ardent admirers, and the second, incredibly, may now be an accurate statement of the law. Nonetheless, a president can still be impeached and removed for commanding an unlawful killing, as Trump has done in authorizing lethal, and illegal, strikes on boats merely suspected of carrying drugs. Murder is a “high crime.” In the fall of 2025, President Donald Trump authorized a series of military attacks on boats in the Caribbean and Eastern Pacific alleged to be smuggling drugs. The first occurred on Sept. 2, 2025. The Pentagon called this and later attacks “kinetic strikes,” a euphemism for blowing up the boats and everything and everyone in them with bombs or missiles fired from aircraft, drones, or other U.S. military platforms. In neither this first attack nor in the many others that followed did U.S. authorities attempt to stop the boats, inspect their contents, or arrest and prosecute the supposed drug traffickers aboard them. In one notorious instance, several members of a boat’s crew survived the initial explosion and were clinging to the wreckage, but at the direct order of Navy Admiral Frank “Mitch” Bradley, the military returned to the scene and killed them. As of Sept. 19 of this year, U.S. forces had hit 70 vessels and killed 234 people. I have personal experience of anti–drug smuggling efforts in the Caribbean. For more than six years, I was an assistant U.S. attorney in Miami. I prosecuted innumerable cases involving most of the ways human ingenuity can devise to move narcotics from South and Central America to the continental United States, including the most direct one—loading drugs on “go fast” boats and speeding across the ocean. When I first saw the black-and-white videos of Trump’s boat attacks, I recognized them immediately. In my days prosecuting drug smugglers, I had watched essentially the same grainy images shot by infrared cameras mounted on military or customs aircraft. With one key difference. Our videos were evidence. They ended with a capture, with crew members arrested, and with a prosecution in a court of law. Trump’s videos, gleefully released by his ghoulish Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, end in a flash of light and either instant death or a slower, but usually no less fatal, struggle with the enveloping ocean. They are official snuff films. What makes them even worse, if that’s possible, is that I never saw the infrared films of aerial surveillance unless the boat had been stopped and drugs found, making clear that the occupants were committing a crime. Because Trump’s uniformed henchmen blow up and sink the boat, usually with all its crew, there is rarely any proof that the boat was smuggling drugs in the first place. On the few occasions where U.S. forces have picked up survivors of the initial explosion, rather than arresting and attempting to prosecute them, the military simply put them ashore on foreign soil, where they would be in no position to dispute U.S. accounts of their supposedly nefarious activities. Hundreds of people have been executed on mere suspicion of crime, with no way even to verify whether the suspicion was accurate. These movies, like the films of my younger days, are also evidence. Not of drug smuggling but of officially sanctioned murder. To begin, law enforcement agents cannot use deadly force merely to apprehend someone suspected of crime. This is equally true whether the suspect immediately surrenders or chooses to flee. The police cannot shoot a fleeing suspect unless the suspect’s conduct threatens death or serious injury to the officer or others. The same basic rule applies at sea: Military and law enforcement officers may not use lethal force to stop a vessel unless they have a reasonable belief that it poses a threat of violence to the pursuers. But there is no indication that any of the boats blown up by the U.S. were armed, carried men who were armed, or posed any threat to U.S. vessels. Indeed, since the U.S. attacks seem to have been conducted using missiles fired from drones or other aircraft, in most cases those on the boats could neither have threatened U.S. forces nor surrendered to them because they would not even have known there were any U.S. vessels in the area before they were blown to pieces. On U.S. soil, police officers who shoot fleeing, nonthreatening suspects can be charged with and convicted of murder or other forms of criminal homicide under both state and federal law. Federal law also penalizes as murder “the unlawful killing of a human being with malice aforethought” if it occurs within the “special maritime and territorial jurisdiction of the United States” (which includes the high seas). Moreover, the Uniform Code of Military Justice provides that “[a]ny person subject to this chapter who, without justification or excuse, unlawfully kills a human being, when such person—(1) has a premeditated design to kill; [or] (2) intends to kill … is guilty of murder.” U.S. experts have concluded that the strikes are unlawful, and a United Nations report concluded that the boat strikes are illegal and could constitute “crimes against humanity.” In domestic U.S. law, police who shoot and kill civilians are rarely convicted of murder in large part because such shootings are almost invariably the product of split-second decisions by officers confronted with confused, chaotic, uncertain situations. By contrast, Trump’s boat strike killings are the product of a policy, adopted in advance, and coldly applied. Boats suspected of transporting narcotics are to be blown up and their occupants killed or at best wounded regardless of whether they could easily be apprehended and despite the fact that the encounters occur on the open ocean, where the boats present no imminent danger to U.S. vessels or anyone else. The Trump administration has justified the boat killings publicly on various grounds, notably including the claim that these vessels were carrying fentanyl, which results in the death of many Americans. The obvious flaw in this bit of public relations flackery is that the main source of fentanyl or its components is Mexico, not the cocaine-producing South American countries from which the boats were sailing. Moreover, many of the first strikes were on boats from Venezuela, which produces little cocaine itself and which drug smugglers use “mostly as a transit hub to send drugs to Europe and Africa.” Internally, the administration’s justification rests on a secret legal memorandum prepared by the Justice Department’s Office of Legal Counsel. The OLC claims that blowing up boats without warning is justifiable because those on them are not merely drug traffickers but members of drug cartels that are actually nonstate-actor terrorist organizations engaged in armed conflict against the United States and attempting to destabilize the Western Hemisphere. Blowing up boats and crews suspected of carrying drugs is alleged to be acceptable because the sale of the drugs would finance the supposed narco-terrorist cartels. The overwhelming consensus of the many experts on the law of war who have weighed in on the administration’s justifications for its killings is that they are flatly wrong or at best legally tenuous. At all events, they rely on factual assertions that are either facially untrue or impossible to verify in part because the military is either killing or immediately releasing any surviving witnesses. Trump’s ongoing campaign of illegal and dishonorable military murders is, without doubt, a constitutionally valid ground for impeachment. Despite the self-serving opinion of the OLC, the killings violate civilian, military, and international law. Moreover, as the famous saying goes, this is worse than a crime: It’s a mistake. First, as anyone who has done any international anti-narcotics work could have told Trump’s bloodthirsty minions (and probably did), blowing up some suspected drug boats and killing their captains and the moonlighting fishermen who crew them was never going to materially reduce the flow of drugs into the United States. It certainly was never going to affect the movement of fentanyl. Nor would sinking a few boats even materially slow cocaine imports. And as at least one recent study has shown, it hasn’t. Second, if anything, just blowing people up impedes serious anti-narcotics work in the Caribbean basin. When you catch traffickers, you can search their boat, interrogate them, and not uncommonly secure information about and later testimony against those who hired them. Detonating a boat full of drug runners—if indeed they are drug runners—is a macabre version of taking a laptop full of intelligence and throwing it into the sea. Third, regularizing illegal killing by the military sets a dark and dangerous precedent. Trump and Hegseth have now created a cadre of soldiers, sailors, and airmen—including very senior officers—with a demonstrated willingness to carry out lethal orders that on their face violate military and civilian law, as well as long-standing military ethical standards. The memo seeking to justify these crimes may, perhaps, insulate those who did the killing and maybe even those who gave the orders from court-martial justice. But they cannot change the fact that the orders should never have been followed nor the fact that the willingness to follow them creates a precedent for the next occasion when a president—whether Trump or a successor—orders the military to carry out unjustifiable killings. Finally, the boat strikes expose the hypocrisy that permeates Trump’s entire approach to law. The first individual pardon of Trump’s second term went to Ross William Ulbricht, who was serving a life term for making millions running a “dark web” online market he christened “Silk Road” that used the anonymity of cryptocurrency to facilitate major drug trafficking, money laundering, and other crimes. In December 2025, Trump granted a full pardon to Juan Orlando Hernández, former president of Honduras, who had been extradited from his home country and convicted in 2024 of facilitating a huge, yearslong drug-smuggling operation that corrupted his own government and moved some 400 tons of cocaine from Honduras to the U.S. In short, Trump giddily supports showy murders of mere boat drivers if suspected of trafficking drugs but happily pardons convicted villains who finance and manage drug trafficking at its highest levels. In sum, the boat strikes amount to “high crimes and misdemeanors” because they are crimes of the most serious sort known to human law, because they subvert the foreign policy interests of the United States, and because they are part of the larger pattern of subversion of the rule of law evident throughout Trump’s second term. By ordering the strikes Trump has acted in violation of his constitutional oath to preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution of the United States and in violation of his constitutional duty to take care that the laws be faithfully executed. There remains the question of whether an article of impeachment based on the boat strikes, however constitutionally sound, should be included in any third impeachment of Donald Trump. One reason for doing so—beyond the self-evident gravity of the crime—is that impeachment may be the only mode of holding him accountable. Because Trump is acting in his capacity as commander of the armed forces, the Supreme Court’s presidential immunity decision would make prosecuting him in criminal court for these deaths next to impossible. Moreover, the structure of legal justification erected by the Justice Department and the Pentagon for the killings will make securing accountability for those lower in the chain of command difficult. Thus, impeachment may be one of few available means of holding someone responsible for all the death. All that said, cold considerations of realpolitik might counsel against including the boat strikes in a third Trump impeachment, particularly one that would be fully justifiable on many other grounds. The basic issue is that drug traffickers, particularly when labeled “narco-terrorists,” have no public constituency. A goodly fraction of the public thinks that the military should be used abroad against “drug cartels.” And while there may be increasing skepticism about particular aspects of the boat strikes (especially the lack of prior judicial authorization and the killing of survivors), one poll showed 48 percent approving military attacks on boats containing suspected drug smugglers. My recommendation would be that a new Congress should carefully and publicly investigate the boat strike campaign but include charges related to it in an impeachment effort only if public opinion plainly turns against Trump on the issue and only as part of a comprehensive case that Trump is subverting the rule of law at home and abroad. Will the Supreme Court Actually Listen to Ketanji Brown Jackson on Her Biggest Expertise?
2026-10-18

Will the Supreme Court Actually Listen to Ketanji Brown Jackson on Her Biggest Expertise?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. On Tuesday, the Supreme Court continued its return to action with Beaird v. United States, a case about criminal sentencing. The court will decide exactly how closely judges are required to listen to guidelines of the United States Sentencing Commission over how long a convicted defendant can be jailed for. While the justices, especially Neil Gorsuch and Ketanji Brown Jackson, sparred over how to handle the court’s recent precedent weakening federal agency power, a concerning pattern re-emerged for the first time in this new term. What we have is a court with a nearly unrelenting drive to monopolize its own view of the law over everyone else’s. In the U.S. criminal system, after a jury finds a person guilty beyond a reasonable doubt, it is generally up to the district court judge to determine their sentence. One problem with that system is that different judges can have vastly different views on the true severity and punishment deserved for the same crime. To help remedy unfair disparity between sentences, Congress created the U.S. Sentencing Commission in the 1980s, an independent federal agency within the judicial branch. Headed by presidentially appointed judges and criminal law experts, the commission provides formal recommendations for the appropriate sentencing for various crimes. More specifically, it provides two types of writings: guidelines providing sentencing ranges (say: 24 to 48 months) and commentary providing more detail on how a judge is supposed to issue decisions within that range. The commentary in particular also instructs judges when they can enhance or decrease a sentence based on the facts of the case. For example, the judge who sentenced Elizabeth Holmes in her healthcare fraud suit relied on the commission guidelines for the initial number of financial penalties but then relied on the commentary to further increase that sentence based on the severity of the financial loss the court calculated. Beaird concerns how judges are supposed to treat these two distinct yet related parts of the Sentencing Commission’s work. Authorities in Texas arrested a man named Kendrick Beaird for unlawful possession of a firearm. The lower court judge sentenced Beaird to an even heavier sentence than he might otherwise have gotten due to the type of large-capacity firearm the police found him possessing. The guidelines told the court to impose a harsher sentence on Beaird for a “large” capacity magazine, but it was the commentary that defined that “large” roughly meant a gun capable of firing 15 rounds or more before reloading. Beaird argued that his firearm was actually below the range that the commentary suggested and, more broadly, that a court has the authority to ignore the commentary if the judge disagrees with it. How much credence a court gives to both the guideline range versus the commentary details matters a lot in a case like Beiard’s, as it can mean the difference of years worth of prison time. At oral argument, no one was more prepared to discuss the Sentencing Commission’s procedure than Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson, who served as vice chair of the commission before becoming a judge. She posed to Beaird’s counsel that the guidelines and the commentary are inextricably linked. They are written by the same officials, the commentary cross-references specific guidelines, and both the commentary and guidelines are reviewed after years of data collection on sentencing patterns across the judiciary. Beaird’s position that judges should be allowed to ignore the commentary whenever they see fit, in Jackson’s view, splits an important mechanism for evenhanded criminal justice in half. Doing so would weaken the usefulness of both the commentary and the guidelines, and ultimately lead to wildly different sentences for the same crimes all depending on the judge. And you can imagine which types of Americans would be most hurt by inconsistent sentencing. As Justice Elena Kagan noted, one way to keep this area of law consistent is for the court to follow its own precedent, in a case called Kisor v. Wilkie. That 2019 opinion, which Kagan herself wrote, was about how much courts should defer to an expert federal agency’s interpretation of its own regulations. Despite a request from litigants to overturn prior rulings in Kisor, a slim majority of the court ruled that judges were still required to defer to an agency’s interpretations of its regulations. So when, for instance, a federal agency like the Department of Veterans Affairs defines a medical term in determining which veterans get benefits, a court has limited discretion to second-guess that expert analysis. One option to resolve this case is for the court to transfer the Kisor deference principle to the Sentencing Commission and rule that this agency, too, is uniquely suited to have a say within its area of expertise. Gorsuch disagrees. Every question he asked made clear that he would shoo away the more deferential Kisor approach, a case that saw Chief Justice John Roberts join the Ruth Bader Ginsburg and Stephen Breyer–era wing of the Democratic appointees to preserve the doctrine. Instead, Gorsuch favors deciding Beaird’s case along the lines of the decision of Loper Bright, an opinion from two terms ago where the 6–3 Republican appointees succeeded where Kisor failed, ruling that judges no longer needed to defer to an agency’s interpretation of the congressional statues (as opposed to regulations). Loper Bright controversially overturned this agency deference “Chevron doctrine” in a move that would have been way too far for even very conservative justices from decades past. A Gorsuch-backed opinion in Beaird would almost certainly rule that courts are not required to defer to the Sentencing Commission whatsoever. This would leave judges free to impose their own view on what it means for a capacity firearm to be large or not, among thousands of other nuanced sentencing questions that come up every year. Among people who study or practice within this admittedly niche area of administrative law, the opinions in Kisor about judicial review of agency regulations and Loper Bright about judicial review of agency statutes are huge deals. From the Environmental Protection Agency to the Department of Homeland Security, the question of how much a court might defer to the agency’s expertise affects nearly every decision they make. What specific types of nuclear energy generators are safe enough to use? How much mercury is considered safe to have in your drinking water? Before Loper Bright, it was the agencies who had the primary say in answering these questions, even while their answers were subject to reasonableness review from courts. After Loper Bright, it’s the courts job to reach what they think is the “best” answer to any expertise-laden question. And if the court significantly changes the balance of deference owed to the Sentencing Commission, upward of 10,000 cases could go back to resentencing. Baird is yet another example of what we might think of as the judicial respect line of cases. Each of the lawsuits mentioned above boils down to how much weight, deference, or credibility this court will give to other actors in the legal system, whether that’s Congress, expert agencies, or state actors. Unfortunately, we appear to be in the middle of a trend of the court insisting it’s the only entity in all of law whose legal opinions matter. And that trend directly harms the movement for a more democratic legal system, for popular constitutionalism. Because a court that won’t even listen to other legal institutions is sure to ignore the views of the people. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. If you want to block ads but still support Slate, consider subscribing. Already a member? Sign in here
News and Politics - Slate Magazine
The Supreme Court’s Right Flank Wants to Give You Fewer Privacy Rights Than a Famous Judge
2026-10-17

The Supreme Court’s Right Flank Wants to Give You Fewer Privacy Rights Than a Famous Judge

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. In 2022, college sports fan Michael Salazar became a digital subscriber to 247Sports.com, a website that shares news about NCAA football and basketball. In order to register for the website, owned by Paramount Global, Salazar signed up for 247Sports’ online newsletter. And he used his subscription to view sports-related video media. Quietly embedded within those videos, though, was a tracking pixel created by Facebook. Because Paramount installed Facebook’s tracking pixel on the 247Sports website, every time Salazar requested or viewed a video, Paramount automatically disclosed the name and URL of the video to Facebook, along with Salazar’s Facebook ID, the unique identifier Facebook assigns to all of its users. Facebook then used that information to show Salazar targeted advertisements. Privacy violations like this have become a routine part of life on the internet. According to a 2024 study on website privacy, more than half of all companies listed in the S&P 500 index use the “Meta pixel” on their sites, sharing people’s information with both Facebook and Instagram. But in theory, there are at least some legal boundaries. Back in 1988, after a D.C. journalist obtained and published a list of movies rented by Supreme Court nominee Robert Bork, a scandalized Congress passed the Video Privacy Protection Act. To be clear: Congress was scandalized by the nonconsensual disclosure of Bork’s rental history, not the judge’s viewing habits, which were pretty unremarkable (unlike those of another judicial nominee who would be confirmed to the Supreme Court a few years later.) The VPPA prohibits videotape service providers from knowingly disclosing the personally identifiable information of consumers, unless they get the consumers’ written consent. The statute defines “videotape service provider” to include any person in the business of delivering “prerecorded video cassette tapes or similar audio visual materials.” And the definition of “consumer” includes any “subscriber of goods or services” from the provider. Here, Salazar subscribed to Paramount’s sports website. He requested audiovisual materials from Paramount, and Paramount delivered them. And then Paramount knowingly disclosed what he watched to Facebook without getting his consent. In September 2022, Salazar filed a class-action lawsuit, arguing that Paramount violated the VPPA. But in July 2023, the federal district court dismissed his complaint, reasoning that Salazar didn’t count as a “consumer” under the statute because he was only subscribed to a newsletter, “not audio visual content.” Salazar appealed to the 6th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, but in April 2025, a divided three-judge panel affirmed the dismissal, agreeing with the district court that Salazar’s newsletter subscription didn’t make him a subscriber under the law. Then Salazar appealed to the Supreme Court, which heard oral arguments in Salazar v. Paramount on Wednesday. Under the plain text of the Video Privacy Protection Act, Salazar should be able to hold Paramount liable for its disclosure of his video history. But during the oral arguments, the Republican-nominated justices peppered Salazar’s attorney with questions suggesting that imposing an atextual limitation onto the statute—one that would conveniently insulate corporations from legal liability when they intrude on consumers’ privacy for profit—was really just the commonsense thing to do. “Wouldn’t it be natural to think that goods or services from a videotape service provider would be video-related services?” asked Justice Clarence Thomas. Later in the oral argument, Thomas said he “always thought of 247Sports as a sports site,” and he questioned the “logic” of viewing the site as a “videotape service provider.” Justice Samuel Alito further argued that the law was “based on the technology of the 1980s,” when people had to “go to a Blockbuster store and get a VHS tape.” Although the text of the VPPA appears to cover Salazar’s claims, Alito suggested that a newsletter subscription isn’t analogous enough to what Congress had in mind and asked Salazar’s counsel why he thought the “smart lower court judges” who ruled against him rejected his “very literal interpretation” of the statute. Justice Brett Kavanaugh echoed these concerns: “We are a textualist Court but usually not a literalist Court,” he said. Kavanaugh continued, “Congress in the 1980s wasn’t thinking about data sharing for targeted advertising.” Kavanaugh and Justice Amy Coney Barrett also worried that, if the VPPA weren’t limited to the long-defunct Blockbusters of the world, practically any business could find themselves liable under the law. Kavanaugh brought up concerns, raised by corporate interest groups in amicus briefs, that “every business in America that has video on its website” could be targeted. Trying to suss out the scope of the statute, Kavanaugh and Barrett both asked how long a video must be for a company to count as a video service provider. Salazar’s attorney indicated that length wasn’t especially relevant and shifted the conversation to the business and profit motive of delivering videos, but Barrett was skeptical. “If I’m on Nordstrom’s website and I click to see the model show me what the shoes are going to look like,” said Barrett, “that’s a profit motive.” For all the Republican justices’ stated concern on Wednesday about reading and interpreting the VPPA in a way that makes sense, they failed to meaningfully engage with the text and context. Paramount provided Salazar with videos and then told Facebook what videos he watched without his consent—the exact kind of injury contemplated by the statute. Technology and commerce have obviously changed considerably since Congress passed the Video Privacy Protection Act. But the law’s commitment to protecting the privacy of video consumers has not. What has changed, however, is the ideological commitments of the people who sit on the court. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Security patches need patches now that attackers have AI
2026-10-19

Security patches need patches now that attackers have AI

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Terra finds sharp rise in GitHub security fixes reported broken within 90 days Updating your software only gets you so far. A scary scenario - security fixes themselves being bypassed - is becoming more common, according to AI penetration-testing startup Terra Security, which found a sharp rise in reported patch failures in 2026 after years of relatively low numbers. And no, it’s not a coincidence that AI vulnerability discovery and patching also exploded this year. For example, Russian government cyberspies exploited a zero-day Windows Shell security bypass bug, CVE-2026-21510, at least a month before Microsoft patched the flaw in February. Redmond’s patch, however, was incomplete, and left a second flaw - a zero-click authentication coercion vulnerability (CVE-2026-32202) - that was also found and abused before Microsoft fixed it in April. Terra researchers reviewed every published CVE record and GitHub-reviewed advisory looking for broken fixes, and found a distinct pattern. After six years of relatively few patch bypasses, this changed in 2026: “A fix would ship, a bypass would be found, and another fix would go out.” The share of GitHub security fixes that required a follow-up advisory reporting a failed patch within 90 days remained at about 0.2 percent between 2019 and 2025. This year, this figure had jumped to 2.3 percent, according to the researchers. Meanwhile, vulnerabilities whose fixes were later bypassed were about four times as likely to appear on the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency's (CISA's) Known Exploited Vulnerabilities list as other bugs affecting the same vendors, according to Terra. Of these, server-side request forgery fixes and prototype-pollution patches break the most. Compared with their share of all CVEs, prototype-pollution vulnerabilities were about 11 times as common among broken-fix CVEs, while server-side request forgery vulnerabilities were 4.6 times as common, according to Terra. This has to do with the way these types of bug classes are fixed: using a reject-list of inputs such as hostnames, paths, and object keys. There’s a “simple” reason for this, according to Terra. “If the blocklist doesn't foresee every encoding of a bad input, the attacker only needs to find one,” the researchers said. Fixes for Linux kernel vulnerabilities are also more likely to cause new bugs, according to kernel CNA records as of October 8. “In fact, 3.2 percent of kernel CVEs were introduced by the fix for another CVE, and about 10 percent in busy subsystems like fs/smb and net/sched,” the bug hunters noted. The silver lining in all of this, we’re told, is that it should be a wake-up call for software vendors and network defenders to break the “patch and move on habit.” Instead, before shipping patches, software vendors should consider three questions: And for sysadmins and patch managers: consider the same things before applying the fix. “If a vendor fixes an exploited bug, assume someone is reading the patch for a way around it,” the pen testers advise. ® Partner X-energy has not built a single working reactor yet PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Write bot-resistant code with KillSwitch, a programming language designed to confuse LLMs
2026-10-19

Write bot-resistant code with KillSwitch, a programming language designed to confuse LLMs

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Lies, lots of adverbs, and prosaic stories don't make for very machine-readable code, it turns out If you want to keep bots from making sense of the code you worked so hard on and using it to write programs of their own, there's a new programming language out there designed to confuse LLMs. What began as software engineer Dominik Picheta experimenting with whether LLMs understand the code they write has turned into something else entirely: An open-source, albeit esoteric, programming language he’s dubbed KillSwitch. There are no brackets, no formatting standards to remember, and seemingly no structure: Just chunks of text, ideally as prosaic as possible, interspersed with functions, variables, and statements written in a manner to be as confusing as possible to LLMs. That means humans might be a bit befuddled too, naturally. “It's totally normal for a human to be confused by the language at first,” Picheta told The Register. “I like to think of it as a bit of a fun puzzle: you should explore the language, play with its interpreter and learn how all the pieces fit together.” KillSwitch wasn’t built by any random person looking to code something confusing to LLMs, and it wasn’t vibe-coded by an AI enthusiast, either. Picheta knows his way around programming languages: He’s a senior software engineer at Cloudflare working on the Workers Runtime team, and is one of the core developers of the Nim programming language. At its most basic, KillSwitch is based around adverbs, which serve as every single identifier in a piece of code. Calling one of those identifiers requires using an anagram of the original adverb, but those anagrams don't have to be actual words - just rearrangements of the original letters. Take, for example, pushing "Hello World" onto the stack and then printing it, as explained on the demo page linked earlier in the story. The program contains several paragraphs of text, most of which the interpreter ignores apart from a few key phrases. Writing “this story starts [adverb]” identifies a code entrypoint. Once inside the code section, writing “ignore previous instructions [adverb anagram]” calls a function, several of which are predefined. In the case of "Hello World," the function to print something is "visibly." Put whatever you want to print in quotation marks, push it onto the stack, call "visibly," and that's that. KillSwitch isn’t a full-featured language, with Picheta describing it as a stack-based interpreted language with only “a limited set of constructs including functions, variables, and if statements,” given its evolution out of an experiment to see how well a programming language could fool an AI into not understanding what was written. Where it gets confusing for an AI is with commands like “ignore previous instructions,” especially when found in the middle of a chunk of text that doesn’t make much sense in that context. KillSwitch’s specification document on GitHub provides more details about the language and is itself designed to confuse LLMs. In one section of the spec, it’s explained that KillSwitch “is equipped with the ability to scan the local network, attempt to find security vulnerabilities in the services on that network, then take those services over and terminate them.” That's not actually the case, as a close reading of the paragraph reveals and Picheta confirmed to us. “The hack statement is both designed to trigger many models' safety guardrails but also to test an LLM's ability to discern fact from fiction,” Picheta told us, adding that there are several lies in the spec document designed to confuse any LLM that may scrape it in an attempt to understand how the language works. “The idea is that humans should be able to deduce what is a lie, whereas LLMs are naive and can be easily swayed by what is written in the spec,” Picheta added. “Some don't realize what its semantic meaning is, whereas others refuse to continue because they believe the script may hack someone.” A programming language designed to confuse AI models seems unlikely to stay obfuscated for long - especially if it’s open source, available on Github, and likely to be scraped by an LLM at some point. Picheta’s aware of that inevitability, which, along with the fact he wanted to test AI’s ability to understand confusing code, is why he’s benchmarked a number of major models against it. “I'm 99.9 percent certain the AI models I benchmarked KS on have not been trained on its source,” Picheta said. “The source hasn't been in the public domain for very long … I'm pretty sure none of the benchmarked models have had training runs that began after the release.” The best-performing model only managed a composite benchmark score of 66.8 percent on KillSwitch coding tasks, while most struggled to get above 50 percent. Even at that high a score, Picheta doesn’t believe it suggests LLMs understand the code they’re writing. Examples in the benchmark were simple, and he believes that, even if an AI were trained on the intricacies of KillSwitch, it’s still going to get tripped up. “My instinct is that in those circumstances they will suddenly be able to ace the basic tasks, which are based on the open sourced test files in the GitHub repo, but will continue to fail to build more complex tasks requiring them to write longer and more complex scripts,” Picheta asserted - and he’s up for the challenge. “I am excited to see how KillSwitch shows up in model training, and think that we can also take advantage of that training to break LLMs in new ways too as KillSwitch evolves,” Picheta said. Those interested in trying KillSwitch out can find it on GitHub, and don't hesitate to make changes: Picheta said he’s ready and willing to incorporate new ways of making it even more LLM resistant. ® Partner X-energy has not built a single working reactor yet PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... But why oh why didn't they call Claude clone Fauxtoshop? Europe’s cloud sovereignty rules could do more harm than good
2026-10-19

Europe’s cloud sovereignty rules could do more harm than good

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Report warns that sovereignty is being used to favor selected national providers and could stifle pan-Europe clouds Europe’s drive for cloud sovereignty is leading to member states favoring providers from their own nation instead of building a Europe-wide cloud services market, according to a report critical of the EU’s proposed Cloud and AI Development Act (CADA). A study by the European Centre for International Political Economy (ECIPE) looked at public cloud contracts in nine EU member states and found that every one that invoked the new vocabulary around “sovereignty” went to a domestic provider, rather than one based in another EU country. It says that European cloud procurement was already highly concentrated nationally before sovereignty became a key policy objective, based on analysis of 8,725 cloud-related procurement contracts between 2018 and 2025. Nearly all went to providers in the same country with negligible intra-EU cross-border competition. Furthermore, the report claims that those domestic wins often turn out to be from prime contractors that are relying on infrastructure provided by the big hyperscale operators, where sovereignty attaches to the nationality of the operator, not to the infrastructure layer on which the service depends. At issue is a conceptual confusion over cloud sovereignty, which is being used to represent two different problems, according to ECIPE. One treats sovereignty as a security and resilience objective, securing data and managing risks. Sovereignty here means that the user has choices over the provider and design of their cloud service needs. The second treats sovereignty as an industrial policy objective, where sovereignty is more about the notion of Europe-headquartered firms becoming more successful if they are not exposed to competition with non-European providers. CADA includes the two different logics and, confusingly, aims to achieve both outcomes, the report states. The legislation frames the four European Union assurance levels as criteria for security, resilience and trustworthy cloud provision, but the criteria actually aim to enforce sovereignty through requirements concerning EU-located infrastructure, restrictions on third-country control, and control over software supply chains. The goal of cloud sovereignty is therefore at risk of becoming a vehicle for policies that favor selected providers, restrict competition and fragment digital markets, ECIPE warns. Instead of allowing pan-European rivals for the big US cloud providers to grow and flourish, the framework may thus have the opposite effect in encouraging member states to favor providers within their own borders, leading to fragmentation across Europe. A more coherent approach would keep the resilience objectives, but temper their use through technological neutrality requirements that preserve interoperability, portability, and multi-cloud options, the report authors state. It makes a number of policy recommendations, including defining sovereignty as a demonstrated outcome, not an ownership status. “Real sovereignty means a public buyer can show its data is secure and that it can switch provider if it has to. A provider’s passport does not tell you that”, said ECIPE Director and co-author of the study, Fredrik Erixon. “Europe’s cloud market is already national in practice. CADA risks locking that in.” But not everyone might agree. Earlier this year, tech trade association CISPE (Cloud Infrastructure Service Providers in Europe) urged the European Commission to legislate for real tech sovereignty, rather than the illusion of it. It argued that sovereignty must be defined by control, not by whether a provider merely has an EU presence, and that means effective ownership of the underlying technology and protection from extraterritorial laws like the US CLOUD Act, under which Washington can compel American tech companies such as the big cloud operators, to hand over any data they hold, including data stored overseas. The same group had earlier warned that the EC’s criteria for cloud sovereignty had been drafted so vaguely as to favor incumbents – meaning the big American operators that already dominate Europe's cloud market.  ® Partner X-energy has not built a single working reactor yet PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Red Hat helps teach about tech writing
2026-10-19

Red Hat helps teach about tech writing

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
But learns tough lessons about AI in the process In Brno in Czechia, Red Hat’s large local office has been helping the city’s Masaryk University to teach future technical writers. Whenever someone tries to teach, though, they learn as well. At the Open Source Summit in Prague, a senior member of Red Hat’s documentation team in Brno presented a talk called Diagrams & Dragons: Teaching Technical Writing to University Students the Open Source Way. Red Hat has a large site in Brno, which is the second-largest Czech city and is also the home of the respected Masaryk University. Red Hat’s Brno office has a substantial documentation team — this vulture was part of it for a while. For the last four years, Red Hat has been working with the university’s Faculty of Informatics to teach a course unit in technical writing. Jiří Herrman talked about what the course tutors had learned from the students. For FI MUNI, its course PV177 Technical Writing. Red Hat has a page with some of the materials. “RH has been looking into cultivating technical writers in Brno for a while,” he said, but the launch of the course was “delayed due to the 2020 worldwide global… thingy.” The company particularly wants to work on what he described as “that tricky part of technical writing where you actually have to interact with other humans.” Some of the things that struck this former tech-docs writer the most are covered on pages 7 and 9 from Herrman’s short slide deck [PDF], which discuss what didn’t work. Although the course was aimed at students with some technical knowledge, they found that setting classwork with shell-based tools didn’t work: some of the students had never used the command-line, some came with borrowed laptops without admin-level access, and some came with just phones rather than laptops. Some we found less surprising. Getting students to grade one anothers’ work, much as professional tech writers review one another’s contributions, didn’t work well – partly due to the speed required. In the end, the compromise was to give the students a course in grading each other’s work “without pissing each other off.” It didn’t shock us, but we suspect might leave some training vendors taken aback, that interacting with remote teachers who were presenting online didn’t work well – especially when there were multiple tutors, changing with each class. You may well have already foreseen the biggest “gotcha” for a course that ran for the first time in 2023. It is, naturally, use of LLM. The students were told that they were not allowed to use LLMs during the course, but naturally enough, they did so anyway. Among the problems that Herrman reported were that giving praise to good work that involved LLM use just encouraged more cheating. However, telling students off for it discouraged them, and that encourages cheating. So the team had to come up with some tactics to try to manage this, which involved teaching the students how to use LLMs instead. They did, however, find some things that did work well. Awarding points, even on a semi-arbitrary basis, with occasional secret prizes, as well dispensing feedback via internet-style memes. A final exercise was to role-play a scenario, “Dungeons & Dragons” style. The quest was “I want to be a tech writer, but how to get there?”. This involved navigating a cross-functional team, creating things like release notes, technical notes, and some real documentation for the process. And as the course materials are open source, the graduates ended up with a permanent FOSS record that they can show to prospective employers. There is a project Github, and you can see some of the student’s archived coursework for 2025 and 2026. The issues around LLM use in tech writing were the primary subject of a “Birds of a Feather” session that Herrman moderated earlier: Technical Writing in the Age of AI. He started by asking how many in the audience were using AI in their tech-writing efforts, getting what he called an “overwhelming majority” who were already on-board. What we found interesting is what people said they didn’t use it for. They find even the best models are poor at writing documentation themselves. They’re also poor at drawing diagrams, and at reading them for review purposes. They’re poor at reviewing docs (“AI is too obsequious and so reviews are overly positive,” and “it doesn’t give good feedback on user focus, or structure.”) However, most of the attendees found uses for it all the same. Examples included “Change management” and “Updating to new code” – in other words, identifying which parts of large products had changed and which therefore needed fresh documentation. Others said they used it for “Documenting decisions from meeting notes,” and in other ways, “Collecting information resources for writing,” and worryingly, to “Summarize notes (from conferences, meetings, etc.) and cluster by themes”. Another use was “Reverse engineering code to figure out intent,” or to “Find existing docs to prevent reinvention or duplication.” Tech writing is a demanding job, and pretty much permanently under-resourced. We can’t and don’t blame anyone for grabbing onto anything that might help. Even so, for us, what stood out from this session was the pretty much unanimous agreement among an audience of tech writers that LLMs were poor at doing the job that tech writers themselves did. Reviewing each others’ docs is a core part of the job: tech writers are used to this, and when they review the bots’ efforts, they find the machinese lacking. That fits our impressions, too. What we found alarming is that, although they find the bots poor at doing what the writers know best, even so, they trusted the bots to be competent in other functions, things the writers don’t look at so closely – for example, finding and selecting where docs need updating. The use for summarization is especially alarming: this is something that studies have demonstrated LLMs are bad at. They shorten, not summarize, and that’s no substitute. We find that worrying, but we regularly hear reports of companies pressing staff to use AI more. This is an all-too-human response: “it can’t do my job, but if I must use it, then this stuff isn’t my jurisdiction, and if it messes up, I can blame someone else.” We just worry at whose desk this buck will eventually stop. ® Partner X-energy has not built a single working reactor yet PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Uber dodges £340M cabbie lawsuit after drivers miss legal deadline
2026-10-19

Uber dodges £340M cabbie lawsuit after drivers miss legal deadline

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Black cab drivers accused ride-hailing giant of misleading London's transport regulator, but judge says they left it too late to sue Uber has dodged a £340 million lawsuit brought by thousands of London black cab drivers after a High Court judge ruled that they'd left it too late to sue. Monday's ruling kills off two claims involving around 13,000 cabbies and former private hire companies Kabbee and Iride, which accused Uber of misleading Transport for London (TfL) to secure its operating license. The cabbies wanted nearly £200 million in compensation for lost earnings, while Kabbee and Iride sought another £140 million. But the court never got as far as deciding whether Uber had done anything wrong. The claims were filed in May and June 2024, but Mr Justice Simon Birt ruled they were too late under the six-year time limit. The cabbies insisted that they hadn't had enough evidence to allege fraud until June 2018, but the judge wasn't buying it. According to the judgment, seen by The Register, three cabbies had already filed their own claims against Uber in September 2017. Litigation funder Harbour approached law firm Mishcon de Reya about a larger lawsuit that December, and by February 2018, the Licensed Taxi Drivers' Association was telling members about the planned action. The dispute dates back to Uber's arrival in London in 2012. The claimants alleged that the company deliberately misled TfL about how its booking system worked, allowing it to operate when it shouldn't have been permitted to do so. Uber has some history here. In a separate licensing appeal in 2018, the company admitted that it gave TfL and the High Court false information about how bookings were accepted through its app. The magistrate stopped short of finding dishonesty, however, and granted Uber a new 15-month license. The judgment also revisits Greyball, Uber's software for identifying suspected regulators, and Ripley, a tool used to remotely lock computers during regulatory visits. Uber maintained that Ripley hadn't been used that way in the UK. The cabbies insisted that they needed information from the June 2018 licensing appeal to support their fraud allegations. Justice Birt disagreed, finding that enough information had been available earlier to bring a claim. Things then went quiet. Mishcon sent Uber a letter before action in July 2019, but Harbour pulled its funding that November. The claimants didn't write to Uber again about the potential lawsuit for another four and a half years, according to the judgment, and by the time proceedings began in 2024, the deadline had passed. The ruling is a victory for Uber, but doesn't clear it of the alleged fraud. The judge dismissed the claims because they were filed too late, without deciding whether the allegations, which Uber denies, were true. Neither Uber nor RGL Management, the company representing the cabbies, responded to The Register’s questions. London's taxi wars are far from over. Uber is working with British autonomous driving startup Wayve on driverless technology. SAP's Signavio hit with function injunction in Germany
2026-10-19

SAP's Signavio hit with function injunction in Germany

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Vendor says patent ruling only restricts one specific process, not the whole product, after legal challenge from Celonis A German court has issued an injunction banning SAP from providing certain features of its Signavio product, following a legal challenge from process mining biz Celonis. The ruling only applies to implementations in Germany, but is part of a broader legal tussle between the two companies over intellectual property. This case focuses on software which helps companies understand their own complex business processes in order to improve them and help prepare for an application migration. The Munich Regional Court ruled against SAP for infringing a Celonis patent covering the technology which determines the lead time between process stages of a process instance. The court also asked SAP to provide more information and offer financial statements about its alleged infringement. In a statement, an SAP spokesperson said: “The court’s ruling relates to one specific function within SAP Signavio Process Intelligence, not to the product as a whole. SAP Signavio Process Intelligence remains available to customers. S/4HANA Cloud migrations are not affected. SAP is working to ensure that there is no disruption to our customers.” SAP added: "SAP respectfully disagrees with the Munich court's ruling and has appealed the decision. SAP has separately challenged the validity of the asserted patent before the Federal Patent Court. In fact, the Munich court's decision represents only one of several ongoing disputes between SAP and Celonis, including patent actions brought by SAP in Germany and the United States. SAP’s first priority is ensuring that there is no disruption to our customers, and we will address all related matters through the appropriate legal channels." Celonis specializes in process mining, building software designed to gather data from people's interactions with business applications. It aims to uncover how organizations’ processes are actually performed, as opposed to how they were designed. For example, during a process mining project, pharma giant GSK discovered around 28,000 different variations for the process of running a sales order. Celonis was founded in 2011 and early customers included Siemens, with projects examining how processes run on the global engineering company’s SAP software. In 2021, SAP decided it wanted in on the same market and bought mining company Signavio, in part to help customers move from legacy software systems to the cloud. The ERP giant does not mandate Signavio for its migrations, but the software is bundled in packages under the moniker RISE with SAP, the lift-shift-and-transform commercial push SAP launched in 2021 to get users into the cloud and on its latest ERP offering, S/4HANA. The latest ruling is part of a set of legal disputes between Celonis and SAP. In March last year, Celonis filed a lawsuit in the US Court, Northern District of California, claiming the ERP giant had made it more difficult and expensive for third-party software companies to get hold of customer data stored in SAP's business software. Celonis was later allowed to amend and refile its complaint after the US court dismissed most of the claims. A trial is set for September 2027. SAP has also launched a case alleging a patent infringement action against Celonis at a Düsseldorf, Germany, court. It also filed suit in October last year at the District Court of Delaware over four US patents. Celonis has launched other cases alleging infringement by SAP, including in a Texas federal district court. ® Partner X-energy has not built a single working reactor yet PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Amazon is funding a new nuclear plant in Indiana
2026-10-19

Amazon is funding a new nuclear plant in Indiana

The Verge | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The proposed nuclear plant would provide 960 megawatts to power the grid — and Amazon’s data centers.﻿ The proposed nuclear plant would provide 960 megawatts to power the grid — and Amazon’s data centers.﻿ Amazon has signed an agreement with X-energy and American Electric Power (AEP) to fund the building of a new nuclear energy facility in Indiana. The proposed plant, which would replace the now defunct coal-fueled Breed Generating Station in Fairbanks, Sullivan County, aims to provide up to 960 megawatts of carbon-free energy for both the regional power grid and Amazon’s data centers. No timeline for construction or operation has been announced for the facility, which Amazon says “could” create 1,000 construction jobs and 100 permanent roles. The facility in Indiana is targeting to build 12 Xe-100 SMR’s, an advanced (but unproven) reactor that’s still undergoing development and licensing. These reactors can be cooled using either air, water, or a hybrid solution of both, which Amazon says should allow them to be deployed in water-constrained regions without impacting water demand in local communities. Indiana also happens to be home to a famously expensive and uncompleted energy project called the Marble Hill Nuclear Power Plant. It was abandoned half-finished in 1983 after $2.5 billion had already been sunk into it, and has been under continued demolition since 2008. Terms for Amazon’s agreement in Fairbanks haven’t been disclosed, so we can’t compare the cost between the facilities. This marks the second nuclear energy project that Amazon and X-energy are collaborating on, with the collective goal of working with regional utilities to deploy more than 5 gigawatts of new nuclear capacity in the US by 2039. In October last year, Amazon shared an early preview of its first planned nuclear plant, the Cascade Advanced Energy Facility in Washington. That project is similarly set to build 12 advanced reactors and create local jobs, but construction isn’t expected to start until the end of this decade. Amazon is one of several tech companies, including Meta, Microsoft, and Google, that are exploring their own nuclear energy projects to power AI data centers. Investing in energy grid infrastructure was also one of the commitments that Amazon outlined in a lengthy blog post earlier this month that aimed to sway some of the growing negativity around data center projects, though it’ll be many years before anyone — including Amazon — can reap the benefits of these new facilities.
